The one thing shaping every game on this card

The single structural fact across this card is that every number on it is last season’s, and much of it was set under coaches who have since gone. This is matchday 1 of the 2026–27 Nations League, and ESPN’s standings show 0 played for all twenty sides. Ten of them last played a competitive match in November 2025, four in March 2026 and six at the World Cup; the gap runs from 69 days (Spain, the World Cup final) to 317 (Estonia, a qualifier in Oslo).

The second is who those numbers belong to. Eight coaches take their first competitive match of their current spell: Boštjan Cesar and Sébastien Pocognoli in Ljubljana, John van ’t Schip with Kazakhstan in Tórshavn, Santi Denia and Slaven Bilić (a second spell) in Prague, Rolando Maran and Viktor Goncharenko in Tirana, and Vladimír Weiss (a second spell) with Slovakia in Prešov. Eleven sides carry a division change into tonight: San Marino and Moldova promoted from League D, North Macedonia from League C, Czechia and England from League B; Scotland and Switzerland relegated from League A, Finland, Albania, Kazakhstan and Iceland from League B. North Macedonia–Switzerland is a promoted side hosting a relegated one, in the first meeting of the two. This is also the first League C edition with no relegation. The absences land on the positions the spec weights: Slovenia without Šeško, Scotland without McTominay, Czechia without Schick (retired) and four injured defenders, England without Rice, Stones, Reece James and Spence, Switzerland without Xhaka, Embolo, Zakaria, Amenda and Vargas, and Iceland without their first-choice goalkeeper and Sverrir Ingi Ingason.

Structural consequence. Result families are the least readable on the card: the 1X2 is Dangerous on seven of ten, OK only at San Marino–Finland, Iceland–Estonia and Slovakia–Moldova; the Asian handicap is Dangerous on five (Slovenia–Scotland, San Marino–Finland, Iceland–Estonia, North Macedonia–Switzerland, Slovakia–Moldova). Corners, the best-supported family on the 25 September board, are Dangerous on seven of ten here, because on most cards the corner split turns on the level of the opposition and tonight neither side meets its usual level; they are OK only at San Marino–Finland, Iceland–Estonia and England–Spain. Discipline and Team goals are the only families with no Dangerous verdict anywhere: Discipline is OK or split on all ten, each row carrying a published appointment, and Team goals are OK at Albania–Belarus and split on the other nine, with at least one side readable on each. Totals are OK or split on eight, Well-suited at Bulgaria–Luxembourg (the board’s only Well-suited verdict) and Dangerous only at England–Spain. BTTS is Dangerous at Czechia–Croatia, England–Spain and Albania–Belarus; Halves only at Czechia–Croatia and England–Spain. Every card carries at least one Dangerous verdict.

Evidence. No number on this board was supplied to me and none was estimated. Team rates were computed match by match from ESPN’s public match record for the Nations League, World Cup qualifying, the World Cup, play-offs and friendlies with a usable box score — results for goals, over 2.5, BTTS, clean sheets and failed-to-score; per-match box scores for corners, fouls, cards and shots; key-event periods for first-half goals. League baselines are in baselines_0926.md and were recomputed from ESPN’s record of the 2024–25 edition of each division — League A, League B and League C, each matching its baselines_0924.md or baselines_0925.md entry — with zero baseline searches, because 2026–27 has no completed match in any of today’s groups; the context lines for promoted and relegated sides beneath them were computed the same way. Fields unpublished across the slate: match-level xG for national teams — ESPN carries none for internationals, and the xG on the board is FotMob’s competition totals (qualifying, and the World Cup where played), printed as totals; transfer fees have no national-team equivalent, and squad turnover is read from call-ups, injuries and retirements against each side’s competitive starts since September 2024. ESPN’s officials field was still empty for all ten matches when the cards were written; every appointment on the board was taken from Wikipedia’s division pages citing UEFA’s match pages, most with a second source. Starting elevens were in ESPN’s record before writing only for Slovenia–Scotland and Iceland–Estonia. For part of this session the environment’s network policy blocked ESPN and Wikipedia: Iceland–Estonia was first built without them and then rebuilt in full, and Czechia–Croatia was re-read from ESPN before it was finished, so no card on this board rests on the blocked build. Last season standing in for a thin current sample: everywhere — every strip is 24/25 and 25/26 with match counts in brackets, because the 26/27 sample is zero for all twenty sides.
Completion. 10 of 10 cards are built, nine at full T1 depth and one at reduced T2 depth (f4 Bulgaria – Luxembourg: sections A, C and D only). Nothing was listed without research. Triage set eight fixtures at T1 and two at T2 on capacity, and f3 Faroe Islands – Kazakhstan was moved to T1 in Phase 2 when its first search found Kazakhstan’s coach change. The depth of each card follows the evidence available and the capacity, not merit, and a reduced card is not a judgement on the game.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Slovenia stay in League B: third in Group B3 in 2024–25 on 8 points, 7–9 (W2 D2 L2, behind Norway and Austria). They then kept their place in the B/C play-off against Slovakia, 0–0 in Bratislava and 1–0 after extra time in Ljubljana (0–0 at 90 minutes). Scotland are relegated from League A: third in Group A1 on 7 points, 7–8 (W2 D1 L3, behind Portugal and Croatia). They then lost the A/B play-off to Greece, 1–0 in Piraeus and 0–3 at Hampden. In World Cup qualifying Slovenia were third in Group B on 4 points, 3–8, without a win (D4 L2, behind Switzerland and Kosovo). Scotland won Group C on 13 points, 13–7 (W4 D1 L1, ahead of Denmark), then went out of the World Cup in the group stage, third in Group C on 3 points, 1–4 (Haiti 1–0, Morocco 0–1, Brazil 0–3). The structural headline is two coaches in their first competitive match, each without his predecessor’s top scorer: Šeško for Slovenia, McTominay for Scotland. The eight sides relegated from League A into League B for 2022–23 and 2024–25 took 1.43 points per away match over 23 (W8 D9 L6), scoring 1.52 and conceding 1.35, with over 2.5 in 52.2% and BTTS in 60.9%; four of the eight won their League B group. All eight went down as fourth-placed League A sides; Scotland came down through the play-off, a route none of the eight took. On matchday one seven of the eight were away and went W3 D3 L1, and two of those seven played in Ljubljana (Slovenia 0–2 Sweden, June 2022; Slovenia 1–1 Austria, September 2024). Scotland’s last League B edition, 2022–23, ended with them winning Group B1 on 13 points (W4 D1 L1, 11–5). Slovenia’s League B home record over the last two editions is W2 D2 L2, 9–10. Head to head: five meetings, Scotland W2 D3 (ESPN, Scottish FA). The last two were 2018 World Cup qualifiers: 1–0 to Scotland at Hampden and 2–2 in Ljubljana, where Cesar was sent off in his 100th international (Planet Nogomet). The sides meet again at Hampden on 6 October. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for the 14 competitive matches Slovenia and the 17 Scotland have played since September 2024, set against ESPN’s current squad lists and the two federations’ announcements.

  • Slovenia — coach change; turnover medium, concentrated at centre-forward and on the right. Matjaž Kek left on 1 December 2025 when his contract expired, after the winless qualifying campaign (Wikipedia). Boštjan Cesar, 43, the record cap-holder (102) and a former assistant to Kek, was confirmed by the Slovenian FA’s executive board on 20 January 2026 (search summary of the Slovenia Times, whose page returned 403); his contract terms were not found. Cesar has four friendlies: 0–1 in Hungary, 3–2 in Montenegro, 1–1 with Cyprus at Stožice and 1–2 in Croatia (W1 D1 L2, 5–6), set up 4-4-2 twice, 4-2-3-1 once and 5-4-1 once. Kek’s side lined up 4-4-2 in 9 of the 14 competitive matches, then with three or five at the back in the last four qualifiers (3-1-4-2, 5-4-1, 3-4-1-2, 5-4-1). Out: Benjamin Šeško (12 of 14 competitive starts, 5 of Slovenia’s 10 goals, both of their penalties) withdrew with a shin injury after being named (ESPN, Yahoo, Sports Mole; Planet Nogomet says he still reported to the camp). Petar Stojanović (11 starts on the right) is recovering from knee surgery (RTV SLO). Svit Šešlar (2 starts) was left out on form (RTV SLO). Kenan Bajrić and Marcel Ratnik are injured, and Žan Zaletel and the first call-up Nik Prelec were named and are now listed out (ESPN). Jan Mlakar (5 starts, 1 goal) is not in the squad, with no reason found. In: late call-ups Žan Celar (Basel) and Jure Balkovec (Omonia); returns for Igor Vekić, Tomi Horvat and Mark Zabukovnik (RTV SLO). Players outside the squad made 35 of Slovenia’s 154 competitive starts in the window and scored 6 of their 10 goals. The defence and midfield are intact: Jaka Bijol (14 of 14 starts), Vanja Drkušić (13), Erik Janža (13) and Žan Karničnik (10), Jan Oblak (12) in goal, Timi Max Elšnik (14) and Adam Gnezda Čerin (12) in midfield. Ten of the eleven who started the last competitive match are in the squad. At centre-forward, Andraž Šporar has 6 competitive starts and no goal in the window, Žan Vipotnik 5 starts and 1 goal.
  • Scotland — coach change; turnover high, through the spine. Steve Clarke resigned in June 2026 after the World Cup group-stage exit (search summary of FIFA, LBC and the Scotsman). Sébastien Pocognoli, 39, was appointed on 16 August 2026 on a two-year contract (Wikipedia, Scottish FA, Goal.com). He won the 2024–25 Belgian title with Union Saint-Gilloise (July 2024–October 2025) and was sacked by Monaco on 1 June 2026 after 38 matches (Wikipedia). ESPN’s record has his Union side with three centre-backs in 62 of 64 competitive matches (3-4-1-2 in 23, 3-5-2 in 20) and his Monaco side in 25 of 38 (3-4-2-1 in 17, 4-2-3-1 in 9). Clarke’s Scotland used a back four in all 17 competitive matches, 4-2-3-1 in 14. The Scottish FA named 28 on 15 September, seven of them uncapped (Ure, Welsh, Donovan, Binks, Graham, McPake, McCracken), and 14 who were not in the World Cup squad. Out: Scott McTominay (17 of 17 competitive starts, 5 goals, the top scorer) is recovering from a heart procedure, and Ché Adams (10 starts, 2 goals) is injured (Scottish FA). Kenny McLean (10 starts, 1 goal) and Kieran Tierney (1 start, 1 goal) withdrew injured this week; the uncapped Kristi Montgomery (Blackburn) replaced McLean (Scottish FA). Lawrence Shankland (2 starts, 1 goal) is listed unavailable by Sports Mole and is not in the 28. Craig Gordon (8 starts) has retired; Grant Hanley (14 starts) and John Souttar (10) were not selected; Anthony Ralston (9) is behind Donovan (search summary of ESPN, Sky Sports and GiveMeSport; Pocognoli cites playing style, injuries and lack of playing time). Lyndon Dykes (6 starts, 1 goal) is not in the squad, with no reason found. Players outside today’s 27 made 89 of Scotland’s 187 competitive starts in the window and scored 11 of their 21 goals (one more was an own goal). In the squad: Andy Robertson (17 of 17), John McGinn (14, 3 goals), Ryan Christie (11, 3), Ben Gannon-Doak (11, 1), Billy Gilmour (10, 1; back after missing the World Cup injured), Lewis Ferguson (9, 1), Angus Gunn (9), Scott McKenna (6), Aaron Hickey (5) and Jack Hendry (4). Eight of the eleven who started the last competitive match are in the squad. Oli McBurnie (16 caps, no competitive start in the window) is recalled.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side in any source read. Stojanović’s red card came in a qualifier and he is injured regardless. Slovenia: Šeško, Stojanović, Bajrić, Ratnik, Zaletel and Prelec out. Scotland: McTominay, Adams, McLean and Tierney out. Both sides play four matches in 11 days. Slovenia: Scotland and North Macedonia (29 September) at home, then Switzerland in Lucerne (3 October) and Scotland at Hampden (6 October) (RTV SLO). Scotland: Slovenia away, Switzerland at Hampden (29 September), North Macedonia in Skopje (3 October), Slovenia at Hampden (6 October) (Scottish FA).

  • Referee. Horațiu Feșnic of Romania is named on the Wikipedia page for 2026–27 League B, and a search summary gives his full team of assistants and VAR. playerstats.football lists tonight’s match as his next fixture (labelled there a World Cup qualifier). ESPN’s preview still reads “TBC”, ESPN’s match record carried no officials when this card was written, and UEFA’s appointment page was not read. The appointment is treated as published, not independently confirmed. From ESPN’s record of UEFA club and international matches since July 2024: 14 matches, 4.29 cards per game, 6.41 fouls per card, 0.29 penalty kicks per match and two reds, the same card rate in both seasons (seven matches each). His four competitive internationals, all involving League C or D sides, ran 4.25 cards at 5.76 fouls per card, with one penalty kick. At home in Romania the line has risen: ESPN’s Liga I record, which stops after September 2025, has 3.88 cards at 8.06 fouls per card in 24 matches in 2024–25 and 6.17 at 5.41 in six in 2025–26, with seven penalty kicks in those six (four in Dinamo 4–3 FCSB). playerstats.football (yellows only) has 4.00 yellows per Superliga match in 2024/25 (24), 5.73 (11) or 6.40 (10) in 2025/26 on two profiles that do not agree, and 5.71 (7) so far in 2026/27; his career line there is 1,386 yellows and 37 reds in 316 fixtures, 4.39 per match. Against League B 2024–25’s 4.42 cards, 5.20 fouls per card and 0.27 penalty kicks, his UEFA and international record books at the division’s level and lets more fouls go per card; his recent domestic record books well above it. History: no Slovenia or Scotland match in ESPN’s record or on either playerstats.football profile. No worded account of how he reads contact in the box was found.

Contradictions, recorded and not reconciled: Wikipedia’s League B page lists Slovenia as “promoted from League C” and Scotland as fourth in their League A group; ESPN’s standings have Slovenia third in League B Group B3 and Scotland third in League A Group A1, and those are used. ESPN’s preview predicted a back four for Scotland; Sports Mole and Yahoo predicted the back three that ESPN’s published eleven confirms. Last Word on Sports listed the same twenty-two starters as “confirmed” before ESPN published them. The two playerstats.football profiles for Feșnic give different 2025/26 Superliga counts.

B · Style & early-season reality

Kek’s Slovenia lined up in two banks of four and conceded the ball. They had 43.5% of possession across 14 competitive matches, took 9.00 shots against 11.85 and won 3.07 corners against 4.36; they won more corners than the opponent in 3 of 14. Their matches were low on every count: 0.71 scored and 1.21 conceded per match, over 2.5 in 35.7%, level at 90 minutes in 8 of 14, 2.93 cards per match against League B’s 4.42. They fouled at about the division’s rate (11.79 committed, 10.43 drawn) and were booked at 1.50 per match. The goals ran through Šeško: 5 of 10, both penalties, and the last first-half goal Slovenia scored in a competitive match, in November 2024. The last four qualifiers were played with three or five at the back and conceded three. Cesar’s version has four friendlies behind it, in three different shapes. In the two with a usable box score Slovenia had 48.5% of the ball in Hungary and 33.1% in Croatia, were out-shot 5 to 16 and 13 to 22, and won 1 and 4 corners against 9 and 8. Tonight Šporar leads the line alone in a 4-4-1-1 (ESPN), with no goal in 6 competitive starts in the window; Vipotnik and Celar, not in the eleven, have 1 goal in 6 starts between them. The evidence for Cesar’s Slovenia is four friendlies, and for the attack without Šeško it is the two qualifiers he missed in November 2025: 0–2 at home to Kosovo and 1–1 in Sweden.

Clarke’s Scotland played 4-2-3-1 and let the opponent have the ball. They had 46.1% of possession across 17 competitive matches, were out-shot 9.88 to 16.35 and out-cornered 3.94 to 6.06, but crossed heavily (17.82 per match) and drew more fouls than they committed (13.59 against 11.71). Their matches ran over 2.5 in 58.8% and their own card count was low (1.71 per match), rising to 2.57 away. The goals came from midfield runners rather than the centre-forward: McTominay 5, McGinn 3, Christie 3, with the centre-forwards Adams, Dykes and Shankland scoring 4 between them. McTominay, Adams, Dykes and Shankland are all out. In League B in 2022–23 the same side won 7.67 corners per match against 3.00 and scored 1.83. Pocognoli’s club sides played with three centre-backs in 87 of 102 competitive matches in ESPN’s record, with possession near half (50.7% at Union, 50.4% at Monaco). ESPN’s published eleven fits that: a 3-4-1-2 with Hendry, Welsh and McKenna at centre-back, Hickey and Robertson wide in the midfield four, and McGinn behind McBurnie and Ure. Welsh and Ure are uncapped, McBurnie has no competitive start in the window, and no Scotland side has used three centre-backs in a competitive match since September 2024. The evidence for Pocognoli’s Scotland is a CV and a squad list, not a match.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — both result records belong to departed coaches
Slovenia drew 8 of 14 competitive matches and went W1 D4 L2 at Stožice; Scotland went W3 D1 L3 away with every defeat by one goal. Relegated sides took 1.43 points per away match in League B (W8 D9 L6). Every one of those results was set by Kek or Clarke. Tonight Cesar has four friendlies and Pocognoli none, Scotland start in a 3-4-1-2 they did not use competitively in the window, and each side is without its top scorer in the window (Šeško 5 of 10, McTominay 5 of 21).
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — the margins sit on the draw line
Slovenia’s 14 competitive margins were eight draws, two wins over Kazakhstan (by one and three) and four defeats by two or three (0–3 and 1–4 to Norway, 0–3 in Switzerland, 0–2 to Kosovo). Scotland’s seven away margins all fell between −1 and +2. A family that turns on one goal either way, or on the draw, inherits all of the 1X2 problems above.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — Slovenia’s low line is steady; Scotland’s high line was built by absent scorers
Slovenia’s matches went over 2.5 in 35.7% of 14 competitive matches, 33.3% in 25/26 and 25.0% against 2026–27 League B sides. Scotland’s went over in 58.8% of 17 and 57.1% away. League B’s line is 50.0%; relegated sides away ran 52.2%. The two lines point opposite ways, and the higher one was built by players who are not in the squad: the scorers of 11 of Scotland’s 21 goals are absent, as is the scorer of 5 of Slovenia’s 10.
BTTSOK — Slovenia’s scoring half decides it
Slovenia failed to score in 50.0% of competitive matches, 66.7% in 25/26 and 42.9% at Stožice; they scored 0.62 per match against 2026–27 League B sides, and Šeško, who scored half their goals, is out. Scotland scored in six of seven away (failed to score 14.3%), but with scorers now absent. BTTS ran 35.7% in Slovenia’s matches and 57.1% in Scotland’s away, against League B’s 43.8% and relegated sides’ 60.9% away.
Team goalsSplit — Slovenia side OK, best on the card; Scotland side dangerous
Slovenia’s scoring line is low in every window: 1.17 per match in the 2024–25 League B group, 0.50 in 25/26, 0.62 against 2026–27 League B sides, 0.71 across 14, and the players still in the squad scored 4 of those 10 goals. That is the most consistent line on this card, and the player missing tonight is the scorer of half of it. Scotland’s side has no stable base: 2.17 per qualifier, 0.33 at the World Cup, 1.29 away, with a new coach, a new shape, a front two of Ure (uncapped) and McBurnie (no competitive start in the window) and 11 of 21 goals out of the squad.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — first-half goal lines agree, the 1H result does not
First-half goals ran 1.00 per match in Slovenia’s 14 competitive matches and 1.00 in Scotland’s seven away, against League B’s 1.12. Slovenia have not scored in a first half in nine competitive matches and were level at half-time in 7 of 14 (six at 0–0). Scotland led at half-time in four of seven away. The 1H result carries every 1X2 problem, plus two new set-ups in their opening 45 minutes.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — the team lines sit at or below the division; the official’s recent domestic line books well above it
Match cards ran 2.93 in Slovenia’s 14 competitive matches (3.00 at Stožice) and 3.94 in Scotland’s 17 (4.57 away), against League B’s 4.42; both sides foul near the division’s 11.48. Feșnic ran 4.29 cards at 6.41 fouls per card in 14 UEFA club and international matches, but 5.71–6.40 yellows per Superliga match since 2025/26 and 6.17 cards in ESPN’s last six Liga I matches. The team lines point down or level, his international line to the division and his domestic line up.
CornersDangerous — both sides were out-cornered in most matches, so neither split reads across
Slovenia won 3.07 and conceded 4.36 per competitive match (3.14/4.29 at Stožice); Scotland won 3.94 and conceded 6.06 (4.29/6.86 away). Each won more corners than the opponent in fewer than a third of its matches, so neither record says who takes territory in a meeting of the two. In League B in 2022–23 Scotland won 7.67 and conceded 3.00, the opposite of their recent split, and Pocognoli’s wing-back shape has no Scotland match behind it.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Every figure in the strip was set by coaches who have gone. Kek left in December 2025 and Clarke in June 2026. Cesar’s four friendlies used three shapes, and Pocognoli has no match; his club record points to three centre-backs, which Scotland did not use once in 17 competitive matches.
  • Scotland’s recent numbers come from a higher level. Their 17 competitive matches since September 2024 — a League A group, the A/B play-off, qualifiers against Denmark and Greece, the World Cup — produced 1.29 goals each way per match and a corner deficit of 3.94 to 6.06. Their last League B edition (2022–23) produced a different profile: 1.83 scored, 0.83 conceded, 7.67 corners won against 3.00.
  • The scorers are missing on both sides. Slovenia are without the scorers of 6 of their 10 competitive goals in the window (Šeško 5, Mlakar 1), including the taker of both their penalties; Scotland without 11 of 21 (McTominay 5, Adams 2, McLean, Tierney, Dykes and Shankland 1 each) and 89 of 187 starts.
  • The calendar gap is uneven. Slovenia last played competitively 312 days ago, Scotland 94; Scotland’s last three competitive matches were World Cup games at neutral venues in the United States.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, handicap, corners and the Scotland side of team goals, which all rest on how Pocognoli’s new shape and new front two work. Least sensitive: the Slovenia side of team goals and the first-half goal line, where every window points the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Slovenia team goals are more robust than Scotland team goals, because Slovenia’s scoring line is low in every window (0.50–1.17 per match) and its one change tonight is the absence of Šeško, who scored 5 of its 10 goals, while Scotland’s runs from 0.33 to 2.17 depending on the window and the scorers of 11 of its 21 goals are not in the squad.
  • BTTS is more robust than totals, because both sides’ BTTS evidence runs through one question, whether Slovenia score (failed to score in 50.0% of competitive matches); totals also need a read on a Scotland attack whose 58.8% over-2.5 line was built by players not in the squad.
  • First-half goals are more robust than the 1H result, because both first-half goal lines sit at 1.00 per match against League B’s 1.12, while the 1H result depends on two new set-ups and on Slovenia’s nine competitive matches without a first-half goal holding under a new coach.
  • The team half of Discipline is more robust than the referee half, because Slovenia’s match-card line sits below League B’s 4.42 in every window (2.83–3.33) and Scotland’s in every window but the away split (3.89–4.33; 4.57 away), while Feșnic’s record depends on the competition counted: 4.29 cards in UEFA and international matches, 5.71–6.40 yellows in the Romanian league since 2025/26.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is how Scotland’s 3-4-1-2 plays. The corner split, Scotland’s goals and the handicap all turn on it. The shape and the eleven are published (ESPN, 14:02 CEST); what would settle it is match evidence — a box score from Scotland in this shape, which does not yet exist and which the next three matches in this window will start to supply.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

San Marino are promoted from League D: first in Group D1 in 2024–25 on 7 points, 5–3 (W2 D1 L1, ahead of Gibraltar and Liechtenstein), directly, as a group winner (ESPN standings; Wikipedia’s 2026–27 page). The two wins, 1–0 at home and 3–1 in Vaduz, both against Liechtenstein, were the first two competitive wins in San Marino’s history (Sports Mole). In World Cup qualifying they finished fifth and last in Group H on 0 points, 2–39 (L8, behind Austria, Bosnia-Herzegovina, Romania and Cyprus). Finland are relegated from League B: fourth in Group B2 on 0 points, 2–13 (L6, behind England, Greece and the Republic of Ireland), directly. In World Cup qualifying they were third in Group G on 10 points, 8–14 (W3 D1 L4, behind the Netherlands and Poland). The structural headline is the two-division gap: a side up from League D meeting a side down from League B, with no League C evidence on either side but San Marino’s two qualifiers against Cyprus. The four sides promoted from League D into League C in 2022–23 and 2024–25 (Faroe Islands, Gibraltar, Estonia, Latvia) took 0.71 points per match over 24 (W4 D5 L15), scoring 0.71 and conceding 2.00; at home 1.08 points per match (W4 D1 L7), 0.92 scored and 1.50 conceded; on matchday one they lost all four, 1–13 on aggregate; none finished above third. The seven sides relegated from League B into League C took 1.81 points per away match over 21 (W11 D5 L5), scoring 1.76 and conceding 0.95; three of the seven won their group. In the four League C matches a promoted-from-D side hosted a relegated-from-B side, the home side went W1 D1 L2 (Gibraltar 1–1 Bulgaria, Faroe Islands 2–1 Türkiye, Estonia 0–3 Sweden, Latvia 1–2 Armenia), with 2.75 goals per match and the visitor winning more corners every time. Format: in 2026–27 League C group winners are promoted, runners-up play the last-placed League B sides, and nobody is relegated from League C, because League D is abolished from 2028–29 (Wikipedia; Sports Mole). Head to head: ESPN’s record has four meetings, all Finland wins: 8–0 (November 2010) and 1–0 (June 2011) in Euro 2012 qualifying, then 6–0 in Helsinki (June 2023) and 2–1 at San Marino Stadium (November 2023) in Euro 2024 qualifying. In the last one Soiri scored twice after half-time and Berardi converted a penalty at 90′+7; San Marino had 20.3% of the ball and 4 shots to 14. Sports Mole counts six meetings, all Finland wins; not reconciled. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for the 12 competitive matches San Marino and the 14 Finland have played since September 2024, set against the two federations’ squad announcements.

San Marino’s only League C opponent in the window was Cyprus: 0–2 away and 0–4 at home, 10 shots to 44.
  • Referee. Nathan Verboomen of Belgium is named on Wikipedia’s 2026–27 League C page and in Sofascore’s preview. B · Style & early-season reality

Cevoli’s San Marino have lined up with a back four in all 12 competitive matches (4-3-3 five times, 4-5-1 four, 4-1-4-1 twice, 4-3-2-1 once), and what they do with it depends on the opponent’s level. Against Liechtenstein and Gibraltar they had 48.4% of the ball, took 11.50 shots to 9.00, crossed 16.25 times per match and won 4.00 corners to 3.50. Against the qualifying group they had 28.8%, took 4.38 shots to 22.12 (1.00 on target per match) and won 1.88 corners to 6.12. They foul and are booked at League D rates in every window: 15.00 fouls and 3.17 cards per match (38 in 12), 4.50 cards in the League D group, against League C’s 12.91 fouls and 2.43 cards per team, at 4.74 fouls per card. Their goals come late and rarely: one first-half goal in 12 competitive matches, all five League D goals after half-time, two of seven from Nanni penalties, and more than one goal in a match once in Cevoli’s 24. The evidence for how they play a League C side is two qualifiers against Cyprus (0–2, 0–4; 10 shots to 44, 4 corners to 12) and the League C record of the four sides promoted from League D before them, who won 2.92 corners and conceded 5.58 at home.

Kanerva’s last Finland campaign sat deep: 43.2% of the ball in the League B group, out-shot 8.17 to 15.00 and out-cornered 3.17 to 5.83, with a back five in the last three matches, and 2 goals in six. Under Friis the shape has moved between a back four (4-3-3 three times and 4-1-4-1 once in qualifying) and a back three or five (3-4-3 twice, 3-4-2-1 and 5-3-2), and the five friendlies since used three more shapes: 3-4-2-1 twice, 3-5-2 once and 4-3-3 twice. Possession rose to 47.8% in qualifying and shots to 10.00 against 14.38. Against Malta and Lithuania, the nearest thing in the window to League C opposition, Finland had 52.2% of the ball, 12.50 shots to 10.50, 17.25 crosses and 5.50 corners to 2.50, and went W2 D1 L1. They are rarely booked: 1.07 cards per match on 11.29 fouls, 10.53 fouls per card, 15 cards in 14 competitive matches. Their scoring is thin: 0.71 per match, never more than two in a match, and all three wins by one goal. Pohjanpalo (3, two of them penalties) and Källman (3) scored six of the ten, and both are in the squad. The summer’s change is at the back: Ivanov, Alho, Hoskonen, Uronen and Tenho made 42 of the 154 starts, and Väisänen, in Sports Mole’s predicted back three, has not played for Finland since March 2025. The evidence for Finland against a deep block at San Marino’s level is the 2023 meetings (6–0 with 79.8% of the ball, 2–1 in Serravalle) and the four matches with Malta and Lithuania, one of them lost at home. That is a record of matches, but not of this back line.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — asymmetric: San Marino’s record is the steadiest on the card, Finland’s away record is not
San Marino have won 2 of 24 matches under Cevoli, both against Liechtenstein, and none of the 14 since; they lost all eight qualifiers and both matches with Cyprus, now a League C side. Finland went W1 D1 L5 away and W2 D1 L1 against Malta and Lithuania, losing 0–1 at home to Malta. In the four League C matches a promoted-from-D side hosted a relegated-from-B side, the home side went W1 D1 L2.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — the two margin records point at different sizes
San Marino’s 12 competitive margins run from +2 to −10: three defeats by four, two by six and one by ten in qualifying, but all four League D margins between −1 and +2. Finland have not won a competitive match by more than one goal in 14 (1–0, 2–1, 2–1) and have not scored more than two in any. The 2023 meetings were 6–0 and 2–1, and relegated-from-B sides’ eight League C meetings with promoted-from-D sides ran from −1 to +4.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — every window at this level sits above the division line, but one side supplies the goals
Over 2.5 ran 58.3% in San Marino’s 12 competitive matches (66.7% at Serravalle) and 57.1% in Finland’s 14 (57.1% away), against League C’s 47.9%. Promoted-from-D sides at home in League C ran 58.3%, relegated-from-B sides away 61.9%, and three of the four direct precedents went over. The exception is San Marino’s League D group (25.0%; League D 16.7%). Opponents scored 42 of the 49 goals in San Marino’s matches, and Finland have scored more than two in none of 14.
BTTSOK — San Marino’s scoring half decides it
San Marino failed to score in 58.3% of competitive matches, 75.0% in qualifying and 50.0% at Serravalle; Finland conceded in 13 of 14 and kept one clean sheet (in Malta). BTTS ran 33.3% in San Marino’s matches and 42.9% in Finland’s, against League C’s 37.5%. Promoted-from-D sides at home in League C ran 50.0%, and San Marino scored in the last meeting in Serravalle (a 90′+7 penalty).
Team goalsSplit — San Marino side OK, best on the card; Finland side dangerous
San Marino have scored more than once in one of Cevoli’s 24 matches and in none at home; their competitive line is 0.58 per match on 6.75 shots against League C’s 12.05, and the players still in the squad scored 6 of their 7 goals. Finland’s side has no stable base: 0.33 per match in the League B group, 1.00 in qualifying, 1.25 against Malta and Lithuania, against a San Marino defence that conceded 0.75 per match in League D and 4.88 in qualifying.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — San Marino’s first-half scoring is steady; the 1H result is not
San Marino have scored one first-half goal in 12 competitive matches and none in League D, where all five of their goals came after half-time; promoted-from-D sides scored 0.29 first-half goals per match in League C. First-half goals conceded swing from 0.50 per match in League D to 2.12 in qualifying. Finland scored 0.36 first-half goals per match and led at half-time in 4 of 14. League C’s line is 1.15.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Split — San Marino’s bookings sit above the division in every window, Finland’s far below it; the official books near the division
San Marino were booked 3.17 times per match (38 in 12) on 15.00 fouls, 4.50 in League D and 2.50 in qualifying, against League C’s 2.43 per team; Finland 1.07 (15 in 14) on 11.29 fouls, at 10.53 fouls per card. Match totals ran 5.17 and 2.14 against 4.85. Verboomen ran 4.42 cards at 5.00 fouls per card in 55 matches in ESPN’s record (4.26 in the 2025–26 Pro League), with one international in it, a friendly.
CornersOK — direction consistent at this level, volume not
San Marino won more corners than the opponent in 1 of 12 competitive matches and conceded 5.25 per match (6.12 in qualifying, 3.50 in League D). Finland won more in only 3 of 14, all three against Malta and Lithuania (5.50 to 2.50 in those four). In the four League C precedents the relegated visitor won more corners every time (7.25 to 2.25 per match), and relegated-from-B sides away ran 5.86 to 3.14.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Neither side’s recent line was set at League C level. San Marino’s League D group (Liechtenstein, Gibraltar; a division of 1.58 goals and 7.42 cards per match) and their qualifying group (Austria, Bosnia-Herzegovina, Romania, Cyprus) sit either side of it. Finland’s League B group (England, Greece, Republic of Ireland) sits above it, and their qualifying group mixed the Netherlands and Poland with Malta and Lithuania, both League D sides in 2026–27. The only League C opponent in either window is Cyprus, twice, for San Marino.
  • Both sides last played competitively in November 2025, 312 and 316 days ago. The friendlies since are thin evidence: ESPN has no usable box score for any of San Marino’s four in 2026, and Finland’s five used three shapes.
  • The turnover falls on one side’s defence. Finland are without the players who made 67 of their 154 competitive starts, most of a back line (Ivanov, Alho, Hoskonen, Uronen, Tenho), while both their main scorers remain. San Marino are without 44 of 132, chiefly Contadini on the right, while the scorers of six of their seven goals are in the squad.
  • Most sensitive: handicap and the Finland side of team goals. Both rest on how a rebuilt Finland back line and a thin attack meet a San Marino defence whose concessions depend entirely on the opponent’s level. Least sensitive: San Marino’s team goals, their first-half scoring and their bookings, where every window points the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • San Marino team goals are more robust than Finland team goals, because San Marino have scored more than once in one of 24 matches under Cevoli, and the scorers of six of their seven competitive goals are in the squad. Finland’s line runs from 0.33 to 1.25 per match depending on the window and has never exceeded two in a match.
  • San Marino’s own bookings are more robust than the match card total, because San Marino were booked above League C’s 2.43 per team in every window (2.50–4.50). Finland’s 1.07 sits far below it and Verboomen’s 4.42 near the division line, so the total depends on which of the two team lines tonight follows.
  • San Marino first-half goals are more robust than the 1H result, because San Marino have one first-half goal in 12 competitive matches and promoted-from-D sides scored 0.29 per match in League C. The 1H result depends on Finland, who led at half-time in 4 of 14.
  • Corner direction is more robust than the corner total, because San Marino won more corners in 1 of 12 and the relegated visitor won more in all four League C precedents. Finland’s own volume runs from 2.86 per match away to 5.50 against Malta and Lithuania.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is how many goals Finland score against a side at San Marino’s level. The evidence is four matches against Malta and Lithuania (1.25 per match, including a 0–1 home defeat) and the 2023 meetings (6–0, 2–1). What would settle it is Finland’s first League C box scores, which the next ten days (Belarus twice, Albania) will start to supply.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither side is promoted. Kazakhstan are relegated from League B: fourth in Group B3 in 2024–25 on 1 point, 0–15 (W0 D1 L5, behind Norway, Austria and Slovenia), directly. In World Cup qualifying they were fourth of five in Group J on 8 points, 9–13 (W2 D2 L4, behind Belgium, Wales and North Macedonia; both wins against Liechtenstein). Their last League C edition was 2022–23, when they won Group C3 (W4 D1 L1, 8–6, under Magomed Adiyev, with Slovakia in the group). Faroe Islands stayed in League C: third in Group C4 in 2024–25 on 6 points, 5–6 (W1 D3 L2, behind North Macedonia and Armenia), as they were third in Group C1 in 2022–23 (W2 D2 L2, 7–10) after winning Group D1 in 2020–21. In World Cup qualifying they were third of five in Group L on 12 points, 11–9 (W4 L4, behind Croatia and Czechia), including 2–1 at home to Czechia and 4–0 at home to Montenegro (ESPN standings and match record). The structural headline is the coach change on one side only, on top of a two-level gap in last season’s opposition: Kazakhstan played 12 of their 14 competitive matches in the window against sides now in League A or B, Faroe Islands 8 of 14 against sides now in League C or D. The seven sides relegated from League B into League C in 2022–23 and 2024–25 took 1.81 points per away match over 21 (W11 D5 L5), scoring 1.76 and conceding 0.95; on matchday one they went W5 D1 L1, and three of the seven won their group. At the grounds of sides that had stayed in League C, those relegated visitors went W9 D4 L4 in 17, and out-cornered the host in 13 (baselines_0926.md; Faroe Islands 2–2 Armenia, October 2024, is one of the 17). All seven had scored in League B (2 to 7 goals each, ESPN standings); Kazakhstan arrive with none. Format: in 2026–27 League C group winners are promoted, runners-up play the fourth-placed League B sides in March 2027, and nobody is relegated from League C (Wikipedia’s 2026–27 pages; Sports Mole). Head to head: ESPN’s record has three meetings: 2–1 to Kazakhstan at home (September 2013) and 1–1 in the Faroe Islands (October 2013), both World Cup qualifiers, then 1–0 to Faroe Islands in a friendly on 18 November 2025 at Stadion Aldo Drosina in Pula, Croatia, a neutral venue, for which ESPN has no box score or key events. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent.   • Faroe Islands — coach unchanged since November 2024; turnover low to medium on what is known, concentrated in central midfield; the squad list itself was not found. Håkan Ericson’s spell ended on 16 October 2024 and Eyðun Klakstein, the U21 coach, took over (Wikipedia’s national-team page); he was named permanent manager on 5 February 2025 (the same page and a search summary; his own Wikipedia page cites 17 February). His record is 10 competitive matches, W5 D0 L5, 12–10, from 1–0 in Armenia on 14 November 2024; the first four matches of the 24/25 NL line were Ericson’s (W0 D3 L1, 4–5). Friendlies since the last qualifier: 1–0 against Kazakhstan (Pula, November 2025), 2–1 in San Marino (March 2026), and, in FotMob’s record but not ESPN’s, 0–1 in Estonia (6 June 2026) and 1–0 in Latvia (9 June). Squad: a 25-man squad is reported (search summary), but no list was found in any source reached; the goalkeepers named are Mattias Lamhauge (Strømsgodset), Bjarti Vitalis Mørk (HB) and Bárður á Reynatrøð (Víkingur). Of the eleven who started the last competitive match (Croatia, November 2025), two are named as absent: Andreasen and Frederiksberg.
  • Kazakhstan — coach changed on 7 August 2026; turnover high, at goalkeeper, centre-back and in midfield. John van ’t Schip was announced on 7 August 2026 (KFF: agreement reached, subject to Executive Committee approval, contract to be signed afterwards; its length was not disclosed), with Nurbol Zhumaskaliyev and Yeldos Akhmetov as assistants. He has no match in charge; tonight is his first. He is Kazakhstan’s fourth coach since June 2024: Stanislav Cherchesov (appointed June 2024; contract ended by mutual consent on 27 January 2025, per TASS; six Nations League matches, no goal), Ali Aliyev as caretaker (eight matches in 2025, W2 L6: five qualifiers and three friendlies) and Talgat Baysufinov (from September 2025, per a search summary of Wikipedia; eight matches, W3 D3 L2: three qualifiers and five friendlies, the last on 9 June 2026) (Wikipedia’s national-team page). The 24/25 NL line is Cherchesov’s; the WCQ line is Aliyev’s five and Baysufinov’s three. Van ’t Schip’s last post was Armenia (17 February–6 August 2025, four matches, W0 D1 L3, left by mutual consent), before that Ajax (30 October 2023–19 May 2024, 34 matches) and Greece (2019–21, 26 matches) (Wikipedia). Squad: an expanded list of 41 (Qazinform), then 28 (Sportilinet, 23 September). Out: Alibek Kasym (9 starts at right centre-back, 1 goal) is injured and misses the away matches in Tórshavn and Slovakia; Islam Chesnokov (10 starts, 4 cards, sent off against Belgium in November 2025) is out of all four matches — injured according to the Kazakh press (Sportilinet, Vesti), suspended until 29 September according to FotMob; Dastan Satpayev (Burnley; 3 starts, 1 goal) injured a hamstring on 2 September and is not in the squad. Temirlan Erlanov (Aktobe, defender) has been called in. Players outside tonight’s squad made 87 of Kazakhstan’s 154 competitive starts in the window and scored 5 of their 9 goals; under Baysufinov alone, 16 of 33 starts and 3 of 6 goals. By position: both goalkeepers who started 9 of the 14 (Mukhamedjan Seysen 5, Igor Shatskiy 4) are not in the squad, whose keepers are Temirlan Anarbekov (2 starts, including the last), Stas Pokatilov (2), Aleksandr Zarutskiy (1) and Bekhon Shayzada (none); of the centre-backs, Kasym (9), Aleksandr Marochkin (6, 1 goal) and Marat Bystrov (6, three at centre-back) are out, leaving Nuraly Alip (13 starts, captain) and Sergiy Malyi (3); in midfield Askhat Tagybergen (8 starts, 1 penalty) and Baktiyor Zainutdinov (8, 1 goal) are not selected. In the squad: Yan Vorogovskiy (14 starts, mostly at left-back), Alip, Maksim Samorodov (10, 1 goal), Ramazan Orazov (7), Galymzhan Kenzhebek (6, 2 goals), Bauyrzhan Islamkhan, Islambek Kuat and Malyi (3 each). Six of the eleven who started the last competitive match are in the squad; Kasym, Chesnokov and Satpayev are injured, Bagdat Kairov and Erkin Tapalov not selected.

He was fourth official at the 2025 U21 European Championship and refereed at the 2024 U19 finals (Malta FA, via maltafootball.com). B · Style & early-season reality

Klakstein has used a back three or five in all ten of his competitive matches (5-4-1 five times, 3-4-3 five), where Ericson’s last four had a back four (4-1-4-1 three times, 4-3-3 once). What the shape produces depends on the opponent. Against League A and B sides Faroe Islands had 30.9% of the ball, 6.83 shots to 17.33 and 3.00 corners to 7.83; against sides now in League C or D, 48.1%, 10.75 shots to 14.25 and 4.88 corners to 5.88, W4 D2 L2. At Tórsvøllur they cross more (22.14 per match, 18.79 overall) and win more corners (5.00 per match). Their matches are close and quiet early: 12 of 14 drawn or settled by one goal, five 0–0 half-times, 0.86 first-half goals per match against League C’s 1.15. They foul little and are booked little: 10.29 fouls and 1.64 cards per match, 6.26 fouls per card, no red card in 14. Set pieces carry part of the scoring: three of their 16 goals were penalties and two more were headers, and the preview press describes a compact block built on set pieces (News Directory 3; ASNEWS). The summer change falls in central midfield, where Hendriksson Olsen, Hansson and Andreasen made 16 starts, and in attack with Frederiksberg; the back line of the window (Edmundsson, Vatnhamar, Færø, Davidsen) is not named as absent anywhere. The only evidence of play since November is two June friendlies that are not in ESPN’s record.

Kazakhstan’s shape has followed the coach. Cherchesov’s League B side began in a back five (5-4-1 twice) and moved to 4-4-2 and 4-2-3-1; Aliyev’s qualifiers were back fives (5-4-1 three times, 3-4-2-1 twice); Baysufinov’s used a back four (4-2-3-1 twice, 4-3-3 once), as did the June friendly in Hungary. Against League A and B sides they had 36.0% of the ball, 8.58 shots to 19.17 and 3.08 corners to 7.67, and scored 0.25 per match; against Liechtenstein, 69.2%, 20.50 shots to 3.00, 7.50 corners to 1.00 and 3.00 goals. They foul more and are booked more than Faroe Islands: 13.29 fouls and 2.50 cards per match at 5.31 fouls per card, two reds, and 4.00 cards per match in Baysufinov’s three qualifiers, with opponents booked 0.93. They cross little (11.57 per match). Van ’t Schip’s shape is a CV, not a match. At Ajax in 2023–24 (34 matches in ESPN’s record) he used 4-2-3-1 twelve times, 4-3-3 ten and a back three ten times, with 58.7% of the ball. At Armenia in 2025 he used 4-2-3-1 at home to Georgia (41.0%, lost 0–3) and 5-4-1 in Georgia (35.5%, lost 1–6) in the League B/C play-off; his two friendlies there (2–5 in Kosovo, 2–2 in Montenegro) have no formation in ESPN’s record. At his presentation he stressed discipline and attention to detail (KFF, 7 August). Nothing has been published on how he will line up tonight, and players outside his squad made 87 of the 154 starts behind every figure above.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — the division prior and both sides’ own records point opposite ways, and the visitors’ coach has no match
Relegated-from-B visitors went W9 D4 L4 at the grounds of sides that stayed in League C (17), and W11 D5 L5 away in League C overall (21). Faroe Islands have lost one of seven competitive home matches in the window (0–1 to Croatia) and went W4 D2 L2 against sides now in League C or D; Kazakhstan went W0 D3 L9 against League A and B sides and won only against Liechtenstein. Van ’t Schip has no match in charge, and players outside his squad made 87 of Kazakhstan’s 154 competitive starts.
Asian handicap / DNBOK — the home side’s margins are the steadiest record on the card; the visitors’ were set a level up
Twelve of Faroe Islands’ 14 competitive matches were drawn or decided by one goal, six of seven at Tórsvøllur; their margins ran from −2 to +4, and 3 of 14 were draws. Kazakhstan’s away margins ran from 0 (1–1 in North Macedonia) to −6 against League A and B sides, and +2 in Liechtenstein, and in their last League C edition (2022–23) every margin sat between −3 and +2. The visitors’ side of the margin is the unread half.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — every window at this level sits near the division line; the outlier was set by stronger opponents
Over 2.5 ran 42.9% in both sides’ 14 competitive matches, against League C’s 47.9%; 57.1% in Faroe Islands’ seven at home, 50.0% in their six League C home matches, and 58.8% where stayed-in-C hosts met relegated-from-B visitors. Kazakhstan’s 71.4% away comes from 22 goals conceded in seven, all of them to League A and B sides. No Faroe Islands competitive match in the window had more than four goals.
BTTSOK — the home side’s conceding half is steady; the visitors’ scoring half is not
Faroe Islands conceded in six of seven competitive home matches and in all six League C home matches (BTTS 71.4% and 83.3%, against League C’s 37.5%). Kazakhstan failed to score in 9 of 14 (4 of 7 away), but 5 of their 9 goals were scored by players outside tonight’s squad and 6 came in the two matches with Liechtenstein. Stayed-in-C hosts against relegated-from-B visitors ran 41.2%.
Team goalsSplit — Faroe Islands side OK; Kazakhstan side dangerous
Faroe Islands scored in six of seven competitive home matches (1.71 per match), 1.38 against sides now in League C or D and 1.33 at home in League C; Sørensen, with 4 of their 16 goals, is in Sports Mole’s predicted eleven and named as absent nowhere, and the named absentees scored 2. Kazakhstan’s line runs from 0.00 in the League B group to 3.00 against Liechtenstein, none of it under van ’t Schip, and Faroe Islands conceded 0.75 per match against League C and D sides.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK on the home record; 1H result dangerous
Faroe Islands’ competitive matches produced 0.86 first-half goals per match (12 in 14), with five 0–0 half-times, and 1.14 at home, against League C’s 1.15. Kazakhstan’s 1.36, and the 13 first-half goals they conceded, came almost wholly against League A and B sides (1.08 conceded per match there). Faroe Islands’ seven home half-times split three leads, two deficits and two levels; Kazakhstan trailed at half-time in 7 of 14.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — both match lines and the official sit below the division; the official’s record is club football
All cards in both sides’ competitive matches ran 3.43 per match (14 each), against League C’s 4.85; Faroe Islands were booked 1.64 times per match on 10.29 fouls, Kazakhstan 2.50 on 13.29, their opponents 0.93. Barbara ran 3.36 cards per match in ESPN’s 11 (FotMob: 3.0 yellows) at 9.36 fouls per card on four box scores, and none of the 11 is a competitive international. Kazakhstan’s rate rose to 4.00 in Baysufinov’s three qualifiers, and their most-booked player in the window, Chesnokov (4 cards and a red), is out.
CornersDangerous — the division prior and both team records point in opposite directions
Relegated-from-B visitors out-cornered stayed-in-C hosts in 13 of 17 (5.53 to 3.35), and ran 5.86 to 3.14 away in League C overall. Faroe Islands at Tórsvøllur won 5.00 and conceded 6.14; Kazakhstan away won 2.71 and conceded 7.71, a line set against League A and B sides, against 7.50 to 1.00 in the two Liechtenstein matches. Faroe Islands’ League C home line was 3.33 to 3.83, below the division’s 4.58 per team at both ends.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Kazakhstan’s numbers belong to three other coaches and largely to other players. Cherchesov’s League B line, Aliyev’s five qualifiers as caretaker and Baysufinov’s three qualifiers and five friendlies used shapes from a back five to a 4-2-3-1. Van ’t Schip has no match. Players outside his squad made 87 of 154 starts and scored 5 of 9 goals, including both goalkeepers who started 9 of 14 and three centre-backs (Kasym, Marochkin, Bystrov) who started 21 matches between them.
  • Kazakhstan’s last season was played a level up; Faroe Islands’ was not. Twelve of Kazakhstan’s 14 competitive matches were against sides now in League A or B, and the other two against Liechtenstein, now in League D; their last League C match was in September 2022. Faroe Islands played 8 of 14 against sides now in League C or D and have finished third in both of their League C groups. Kazakhstan’s away concessions (3.14 per match) and first-half concessions (0.93) are League A and B numbers.
  • Both sides last played competitively 315–316 days ago, and the friendlies since are thin evidence. ESPN has no usable box score for any of Faroe Islands’ four friendlies (two are not in its record) or for Kazakhstan’s draw in Armenia, and the only meeting in the window, a 1–0 friendly in Pula, has neither box score nor key events.
  • Faroe Islands’ squad list was not found. The five named absentees made 23 of 154 starts, mostly in central midfield, but whether anyone else in the window is missing is unknown. The back four or five of the window is not named as absent anywhere.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2 and corners. Both rest on a Kazakhstan shape nobody has seen, and on a division prior that runs against both sides’ own records. Least sensitive: Faroe Islands’ margins and home scoring, and the discipline total, where every window, both team lines and the official’s record point the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Handicap is more robust than 1X2 here, because 12 of Faroe Islands’ 14 competitive matches (six of seven at home) were drawn or decided by one goal. The result itself depends on a Kazakhstan side whose coach has no match, and on a division prior (relegated visitors W9 D4 L4 at stayed-in-C hosts) that contradicts both team records.
  • Faroe Islands team goals are more robust than Kazakhstan team goals, because Faroe Islands scored in six of seven competitive home matches and their named absentees scored 2 of 16. Five of Kazakhstan’s nine goals were scored by players not in tonight’s squad, and six came against Liechtenstein.
  • The card total is more robust than corners, because both sides’ competitive match lines (3.43) and Barbara’s ESPN line (3.36) all sit below League C’s 4.85. Corner direction splits between a division prior that favours the relegated visitor (13 of 17) and Kazakhstan’s away line of 2.71 won per match.
  • BTTS is more robust than the 1H result, because Faroe Islands conceded in six of seven home matches and in all six League C home matches. Their seven home half-times split three leads, two deficits and two levels.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is van ’t Schip’s first team sheet: his shape, and who plays in goal and at centre-back in place of Seysen, Shatskiy, Kasym, Marochkin and Bystrov. ESPN’s published elevens will settle the shape about an hour before kickoff. His first League C box scores, in Slovakia on 29 September and at home to Moldova on 2 October, will show what it produces.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

  • Not sourced, and not pursued at this tier. Reduced-depth card: three searches per team plus two on the referee; sections B and E omitted. Fields the tier did not pursue: the reasons most of Bulgaria’s window regulars are out of the squad (Fabian Nürnberger, Viktor Popov, Alex Petkov, Zhivko Atanasov, Radoslav Kirilov, Aleksandar Kolev; only Despodov, Petko Hristov and Chochev have a published reason, and Filip Krastev’s injury appears only in a search summary); the reasons Gerson Rodrigues, Mica Pinto and Edvin Muratovic are not in Luxembourg’s squad; how and when Luc Holtz left; either coach’s stated shape; a style read beyond the possession, shot, cross and corner figures used in section C; any worded account of the referee. Unpublished or unreached: per-match xG for either side (FotMob’s qualifying totals do not state a match count; nothing divided or estimated); box scores for Bulgaria’s four 2026 friendlies (all zeros in ESPN’s record) and Luxembourg’s win in Albania (all zeros, and its goal missing from the key events); none for either side’s 2024 and 2025 friendlies; transfer fees have no national-team equivalent. Pages that did not open: voetbalstats.nl (403), World Today Journal (loading page only). Contradictions, recorded and not reconciled: L’essentiel’s squad list of 18 September includes Korac, and ESPN’s current roster lists Eldin Dzogovic in his place; no source reached reports Dzogovic’s call-up. Korac’s injury itself is not in dispute. ESPN lists Tomás Moreira where the federation list names Tomás Cruz; L’essentiel says they are the same player, registered as Moreira at Benfica.
  • Division and format — neither side promoted or relegated. Both stayed in League C, where they shared Group C3 in 2024–25. Bulgaria were second on 9 points, 3–6 (W2 D3 L1, behind Northern Ireland), and lost the B/C play-off to the Republic of Ireland 2–4 on aggregate (1–2 in Plovdiv, 1–2 in Dublin, March 2025); in 2022–23 they were second in Group C4 (W2 D3 L1, 10–8) after coming down from League B. Luxembourg were fourth on 3 points, 3–7 (W0 D3 L3), and kept their place by beating Malta 5–0 on aggregate in the C/D play-off (2–0 in Ta’ Qali, 3–0 at home, March 2026); in 2022–23 they were second in Group C1 (W3 D2 L1, 9–7). World Cup qualifying: Bulgaria fourth of four in Group E on 3 points, 3–19 (W1 L5, behind Spain, Türkiye and Georgia); Luxembourg fourth of four in Group A on 0 points, 1–13 (behind Germany, Slovakia and Northern Ireland) (ESPN standings and match record). The division prior for this pairing, League C sides that had stayed hosting League C sides that had stayed, is 38 matches: home side W19 D9 L10, 2.08 goals per match, over 2.5 31.6% (baselines_0926.md). Twelve of those 38 are Bulgaria’s or Luxembourg’s own, so the prior and the team records are not independent. Format: group winners are promoted, runners-up play the fourth-placed League B sides in March 2027, and nobody is relegated from League C (Wikipedia’s 2026–27 page, as logged on f2). Head to head: ESPN’s record has five meetings: 4–3 to Bulgaria at home (September 2016), 1–1 in Luxembourg (October 2017), 0–0 in Luxembourg (November 2022), then 0–0 in Plovdiv on 12 October 2024 (11 cards, Bulgaria 6 and Luxembourg 5; fouls 12 and 19; corners 6–4 to Bulgaria; Bulgaria 55.0% of the ball) and 1–0 to Bulgaria in Luxembourg on 15 November 2024 (Kraev, 23′; Luxembourg 64.7% of the ball, 18 shots to 7, 12 corners to 0, 34 crosses; 6 cards). Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for the 14 competitive matches each side has played since September 2024, set against the squads named for tonight.
  • Bulgaria — coach changed on 24 September 2025; turnover very high, at full-back, centre-back and across the attack. Aleksandar Dimitrov, Bulgaria’s U21 coach since December 2018, was appointed on 24 September 2025 on a two-year contract (BTA; Wikipedia), replacing Ilian Iliev, who was released after the 0–3 defeats to Spain and Georgia. Dimitrov’s record to 5 June 2026: 8 matches, W3 D1 L4, 16–18 (Wikipedia): four qualifiers (W1 L3, 3–13, all against League A and B sides) and four friendlies (10–2 against the Solomon Islands and 1–0 against Indonesia in Jakarta in March, 0–1 at home to Montenegro and 2–2 in Moldova in June). Squad: 27 named on 18 September (BNT), with one debutant, Asen Chandarov (Botev Plovdiv); Ivaylo Chochev (Ludogorets) withdrew on 24 September with right-knee pain and has not been replaced (BNR), leaving the 26 that ESPN’s roster lists. Out: captain Kiril Despodov (11 starts, 1 goal) has not recovered from finger surgery; Petko Hristov (2 starts at centre-back) is not called, his transfer delayed and without an official Levski appearance (BNT). Players outside tonight’s squad made 102 of Bulgaria’s 154 competitive starts since September 2024 and scored 5 of their 8 goals; under Dimitrov alone, 21 of 44 starts and 2 of 3 goals. By position: goalkeeper Dimitar Mitov (10 starts) stays. Of the full-backs, Nürnberger (8 at left-back), Popov (7 at right-back), Anton Nedyalkov (4), Martin Georgiev (3) and Nikolay Minkov (3) are not in the squad; Hristiyan Petrov (4 at left-back) is, and Ivan Turitsov returns without a start in the window. At centre-back Alex Petkov (6), Atanasov (5), Simeon Petrov (4), Atanas Chernev (2), Rosen Bozhinov (2) and Hristov are out; Kristian Dimitrov (4), Emil Tsenov (2) and Stefan Velkov (1) remain, with Andrea Hristov, Teodor Ivanov and Yoan Stoyanov (back for the first time since March 2024, per BNT) without a start in the window. In midfield Ilia Gruev (Leeds; 8 starts) and Andrian Kraev (6, the winner in Luxembourg) stay; Krastev (8 starts, the window’s only Bulgarian red card), Georgi Milanov (4), Georgi Kostadinov (3) and Chochev do not. In attack Despodov, Kirilov (9, 1 goal) and Kolev (6) are out; Marin Petkov (5, 1 goal), Martin Minchev (3), Georgi Rusev (2, 1 goal) and Zdravko Dimitrov (2) remain; Lukas Petkov (Elversberg) has one start, and Kristiyan Balov, Borislav Tsonev, Stanislav Ivanov and Chandarov none. Eight of the eleven who started the last competitive match (Georgia, November 2025) are in the squad; Georgiev, Krastev and Despodov are not.
  • Luxembourg — coach changed on 19 August 2025; turnover low in number but concentrated at centre-back and in central midfield. Jeff Strasser was announced as Luc Holtz’s successor on 19 August 2025 (Wikipedia); Holtz had managed Luxembourg from 2010. Strasser’s record to 6 June 2026: 10 matches, W3 D0 L7, 7–14 (Wikipedia): six qualifiers (L6, 1–13), the two play-off wins over Malta (5–0), 0–1 at home to Italy and 1–0 in Albania. The 24/25 NL line is Holtz’s; WCQ and PO are Strasser’s. Squad: 26 named on 18 September (L’essentiel), with one uncapped player, Omar Natami (UNA Strassen), and returns for Marvin Martins, Leandro Barreiro, Olivier Thill, Timothé Rupil and Jayson Videira. Out: Christopher Martins (Spartak Moscow; 11 starts, 4 cards) with a pubis injury; Korac (11 starts at centre-back, 1 goal, 5 cards, sent off against Northern Ireland in September 2025) with a cruciate-ligament injury, out for several months (L’essentiel, 25 September; he went off after 10 minutes for Hellas Verona on 20 September, per Le Quotidien); Olivier Thill (2 starts) reported unavailable with a muscle injury; Enzo Dos Santos a doubt; Mathias Olesen (13 starts, 1 goal) just back from injury and, per L’essentiel, not able to start all four matches. Players outside tonight’s available squad made 40 of Luxembourg’s 154 competitive starts and scored 3 of their 9 goals (Gerson Rodrigues 2, Korac 1); under Strasser, 17 of 88 starts and none of his 6 goals. By position: goalkeeper Anthony Moris (12 starts) stays, and so do Laurent Jans (14 starts, captain, 124 caps per Sports Mole), Dirk Carlson (13, sent off in Germany in October 2025) and Florian Bohnert (12, 6 cards); Enes Mahmutovic (5) is the one remaining centre-back with starts in the window beside Carlson. Midfield keeps Barreiro (13), Olesen and Danel Sinani (12, 6 cards) but loses Martins; Tomás Moreira (Cruz; 5 starts, 16 caps per L’essentiel), Aiman Dardari (8, 1 goal) and Vincent Thill (2 starts, 2 goals) remain. Nine of the eleven who started the last competitive match (Malta, March 2026) are available; Korac and Martins are not.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension reported for either side. Bulgaria: Despodov (finger surgery) and Chochev (knee) out, Petko Hristov not called, Krastev not in the squad. Luxembourg: Christopher Martins, Korac and Olivier Thill injured, Dos Santos doubtful, Olesen short of matches. Both play four matches in 11 days. Bulgaria host Estonia in Plovdiv (29 September), then visit Iceland (3 October) and Luxembourg (6 October). Luxembourg host Iceland (29 September), visit Estonia (3 October) and host Bulgaria (6 October) (BNT and BNR; L’essentiel).

  • Sample. Neither side has played a competitive match in 2026–27: this is matchday 1 and neither played in September before today. Bulgaria at home: W2 D2 L3, 6–13, in seven competitive matches: 1–0 Northern Ireland, 0–0 Luxembourg and 1–2 Republic of Ireland in Plovdiv; 1–1 Belarus, 0–3 Spain, 1–6 Türkiye and 2–1 Georgia in Sofia. Luxembourg away: W1 D2 L4, 3–10: 0–2 Northern Ireland, 0–0 Bulgaria, 1–1 Belarus, 0–4 Germany, 0–2 Slovakia, 0–1 Northern Ireland, 2–0 Malta.   • Referee. Sander van der Eijk of the Netherlands is named on Wikipedia’s 2026–27 League C page and, per a search summary, in WhoScored’s preview. ESPN’s match record carried no officials when this card was written; the appointment is treated as published, not independently confirmed. Born 2 May 1991, Eredivisie debut April 2019, listed as an international referee (Dutch Wikipedia). ESPN’s record has him in 63 matches since July 2024: 205 cards, 3.25 per match (3.08 in 2024–25’s 24, 3.41 in 2025–26’s 32, 3.14 in 2026–27’s 7), 16 red cards, 6.72 fouls per card on 62 box scores, and 10 penalty kicks (0.16 per match). In Dutch league and cup matches in 2025–26 (22) he ran 3.41 cards per match at 6.21 fouls per card with 0.09 penalty kicks; his last three include 9 cards in Ajax 1–3 PSV on 5 September. StatsHub gives 3.28 yellow cards and 0.14 reds per game across 138 matches, and WhoScored 3.45 yellows per match over his last 32 (both via search summaries). Against League C 2024–25’s 4.85 cards, 5.32 fouls per card and 0.38 penalty kicks, his record books less, lets more fouls go per card, and gives penalty kicks at under half the division’s rate; his 16 reds in 63 run above League C’s 8 in 48. Matches between national teams in the record: two, Iceland 5–0 Azerbaijan (World Cup qualifier, September 2025; 3 cards on 24 fouls) and Austria 1–0 South Korea (friendly, March 2026; 1 card on 20 fouls). History: no Bulgaria or Luxembourg match, and no Bulgarian or Luxembourgish club, in ESPN’s record. No worded account of how he reads contact in the box was found.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — the home side’s record belongs mostly to players who are not here, and its coach has no competitive match against a League C side
Players outside Bulgaria’s squad made 102 of the 154 competitive starts behind their record, and Dimitrov’s four competitive matches were all against League A and B sides, at 25.1% of the ball. The prior and the meetings are thin and not independent: stayed-in-C hosts went W19 D9 L10 in 38, twelve of them these two sides’ own matches, and the meetings ended 0–0 and 1–0. Luxembourg lose Korac and Christopher Martins, 13 of Strasser’s 88 competitive starts, and went W2 D2 L4 against sides now in League C or D.
Asian handicap / DNBOK — margins at this level have been narrow on both records
Bulgaria’s four competitive matches against sides now in League C or D ended 0–0, 0–0, 1–0 and 1–1; their −3 to −5 margins all came against League A and B sides. Luxembourg’s eight against such sides ran from −2 (in Slovakia) to +3 (Malta), five of them level or by one goal. Where stayed-in-C sides met, 13 of 38 finished under 1.5 goals. Bulgaria’s new defence is the unread part of every margin.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — every League C window for both sides, the meetings and the division prior sit on the same side of the division line
Over 2.5 ran 16.7% for both sides in their 2024–25 League C group (one match in six each), against League C’s 47.9%, and the two meetings produced 0 and 1 goals. Bulgaria’s four matches against sides now in League C or D had 3 goals in all; Luxembourg’s eight had 12, one over 2.5; stayed-in-C sides ran 31.6% (38). Bulgaria’s 83.3% in qualifying came with 19 goals conceded to Spain, Türkiye and Georgia. Luxembourg’s away matches went over 2.5 in 14.3% (7). What holds this down: the prior contains the same matches, and Bulgaria’s squad has changed.
BTTSOK — the visitors’ scoring half is steady; the hosts’ conceding half was set a level up
Luxembourg failed to score in 9 of 14 competitive matches and 5 of 7 away, and scored twice or more only against Northern Ireland at home and Malta twice. Bulgaria kept clean sheets in four of six 2024–25 group matches and none of six qualifiers; at home they conceded in five of seven, four of those five against League A and B sides. BTTS ran 16.7% and 33.3% in the 24/25 NL group, 26.3% where stayed-in-C sides met, and in neither meeting.
Team goalsSplit — Luxembourg side OK; Bulgaria side dangerous
Luxembourg scored 0.43 per match away (3 in 7), and all six of their goals under Strasser came from players available tonight (Vincent Thill 2; Sinani, Olesen, Moreira and Dardari 1 each). Five of Bulgaria’s eight competitive goals were scored by players outside tonight’s squad (Despodov, Kirilov, Krastev, Valentin Antov, Vasil Panayotov). Their line runs from 0.50 per match against sides now in League C or D to 0.86 at home; Luxembourg conceded 0.75 per match against League C and D sides.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK on the visitors’ record; 1H result dangerous
Luxembourg have scored two first-half goals in 14 competitive matches, none away, and were level at half-time in 8 of 14 (seven at 0–0). Their 2024–25 group produced 0.67 first-half goals per match and the stayed-in-C line 0.84, against League C’s 1.15. Bulgaria’s first halves were busier (1.50 per match, level in 3 of 14), most of it conceded to League A and B sides (1.30 per match). Bulgaria’s seven home half-times split three leads, two deficits and two levels; the meetings were level in Plovdiv and 0–1 to Bulgaria in Luxembourg at the break.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — split: both sides’ match lines sit above the division, the official’s record below it
All cards in Bulgaria’s and Luxembourg’s competitive matches ran 5.14 and 5.29 per match (14 each), against League C’s 4.85; their 2024–25 group matches 6.83 and 6.00, and the two meetings 11 and 6. Luxembourg commit 14.86 fouls per match (17.83 in the NL group) at 4.84 fouls per card. Van der Eijk ran 3.25 cards per match over 63 at 6.72 fouls per card, against League C’s 5.32, with one competitive international. Bulgaria’s own rate fell to 1.00 per match under Dimitrov, in four matches against League A and B sides.
CornersDangerous — the two meetings split 6–4 and 0–12, and both lines were set conceding to stronger sides
Bulgaria won more corners than their opponent in 1 of 14 competitive matches and Luxembourg in 2 of 14; two of those three were the meetings between them, 6–4 to Bulgaria in Plovdiv and 12–0 to Luxembourg in Luxembourg, where Luxembourg had 64.7% of the ball and 34 crosses. Bulgaria at home won 2.43 and conceded 4.57, Luxembourg away 2.43 and 5.57. Stayed-in-C hosts won more in 15 of 38 and visitors in 18.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Bulgaria’s numbers belong largely to other players and to another coach. Players outside the squad made 102 of 154 starts and scored 5 of 8 goals, including eight of the ten full-backs and centre-backs who started more than twice. Iliev had 10 of the 14 matches. Dimitrov’s four competitive matches were all against League A and B sides, in 4-4-1-1 once and 4-5-1 three times. He then used 4-3-3 in all four 2026 friendlies, none of which has a usable box score.
  • Luxembourg’s losses are few but sit where their line was built. Korac and Christopher Martins made 22 of the window’s 154 starts and 13 of Strasser’s 88. Olivier Thill is also out and Olesen is just back. Nine of the last competitive eleven remain, and every Strasser-era goal was scored by an available player.
  • Long gaps, thin evidence since. Bulgaria last played competitively 312 days ago and Luxembourg 179. Bulgaria’s matches since are four friendlies (10–2 and 1–0 in Jakarta, 0–1 at home to Montenegro, 2–2 in Moldova), none with a usable box score.
  • The prior is not independent of the teams. Twelve of the 38 matches in the stayed-in-C line are Bulgaria’s or Luxembourg’s own. Where the prior and the team records agree, as on totals and first halves, that agreement counts once, not twice.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2 and Bulgaria’s team goals. Both rest on a defence and front line that have mostly not started a competitive match together, under a coach whose competitive matches were all a level up. Least sensitive: totals and first-half goals. There, the 2024–25 group, the two meetings, both sides’ records against League C and D sides and Luxembourg’s away line all point the same way.

UEFA · Nations League C, Group C4 · Matchday 1

Iceland – Estonia

18:00 CEST · Laugardalsvöllur, Reykjavík · Iceland are new to League C after A, B and B, relegated through the B/C play-off (1–2 in Pristina, 1–3 in Murcia, to Kosovo). They host Estonia, third in their 2024–25 League C group on 4 points and fourth of five in World Cup qualifying. Iceland are without first-choice goalkeeper Elías Rafn Ólafsson and centre-back Sverrir Ingi Ingason, who made 18 of their 154 competitive starts since September 2024 between them. Estonia are without Sappinen, Sinyavskiy, Anier and five others on health grounds, and Shein declined the call; players outside their squad scored 5 of their 11 competitive goals. Estonia have not won the corner count in any of their 14 competitive matches in that window. Iceland last played competitively 314 days ago, Estonia 317. First League C edition with no relegation; no competitive meeting in seven. Referee Miloš Milanović (Serbia), whose ESPN record is club and youth football plus one League D match. Elevens in ESPN’s record at 17:01 CEST: Stefán Teitur Þórðarson, listed as a midfielder, starts at centre-back for Iceland; Estonia line up 5-4-1 with Karel Mustmaa, who has no competitive start in the window, alone up front. xG is FotMob’s qualifying totals only
