A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Iceland are relegated from League B: third in Group B4 in 2024–25 on 7 points, 10–13 (W2 D1 L3, behind Wales and Türkiye, ahead of Montenegro), then beaten 2–5 on aggregate by Kosovo in the B/C play-off (1–2 in Pristina on 20 March 2025, 1–3 in the listed home leg in Murcia, Spain, three days later). Their division history reads A/B/B/C, so this is their first League C campaign. In World Cup qualifying they were third in Group D on 7 points, 13–11 (W2 D1 L3, behind France and Ukraine). They beat Azerbaijan 5–0 at home and 2–0 in Baku, drew 2–2 at home to France, and lost 1–2 in Paris, 3–5 at home to Ukraine and 0–2 to Ukraine in Warsaw. Estonia stay in League C: third in Group C1 on 4 points, 3–9 (W1 D1 L4, behind Sweden and Slovakia, ahead of Azerbaijan), in their first League C campaign after promotion from League D; their history reads C/D/C/C. In World Cup qualifying they were fourth of five in Group I on 4 points, 8–21 (W1 D1 L6). The win was 3–2 in Chişinău, against ten men from the 5th minute, and the draw 1–1 at home to Moldova; all six defeats came against Norway, Italy and Israel. The structural headline is the opposition split inside both records: 10 of Iceland’s 14 competitive matches and 8 of Estonia’s were against sides now in Leagues A and B. Against the rest, Iceland went W4, 11–0 (Montenegro and Azerbaijan), and Estonia W2 D2 L2, 7–6 (Slovakia, Azerbaijan, Moldova). The division prior for this pairing is not these teams’. In the 17 League C matches of 2022–23 and 2024–25 in which a side just relegated from League B hosted a side that had stayed in League C, the home side went W6 D5 L6, with 3.06 goals per match and BTTS in 58.8%. It won the corner count in 12 of the 17 and was booked 1.88 times per match against the visitor’s 2.88 (baselines_0926.md, recomputed from ESPN on this card). Format: group winners are promoted, runners-up play the fourth-placed League B sides, and no side is relegated from League C (baselines_0926.md, from f2). fotbolti.net adds that Iceland need first place to be sure of not ending in the bottom division, since the Nations League drops to three divisions from 2028. Head to head: seven meetings, all friendlies, Iceland W3 D3 L1, 10–4: 4–0 in Akureyri (1994), 3–0 in Tallinn (1996), 0–2 in Tallinn (2002), 1–0 in Reykjavík (2014), 1–1 in Tallinn (2015), 0–0 in Doha (2019) and 1–1 in Albufeira (2023). ESPN’s last five and Sekundomer’s count agree. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for all 14 of each side’s competitive matches, set against the squads the two federations published.

  • Iceland — coach Arnar Gunnlaugsson, appointed 15 January 2025; turnover medium, concentrated at goalkeeper and centre-back. Arnar named 24 on 16 September and has taken 8 competitive matches (the play-off and qualifying). Out since: goalkeeper Elías Rafn Ólafsson (Midtjylland; 6 starts, both 2025 clean sheets), injured in a club match and replaced by Anton Ari Einarsson (KSÍ, 20 September). He is on the injury list for the coming months (fotbolti.net); Sports Mole says he has had kidney surgery. Bjarki Steinn Bjarkason broke a bone in his foot and was not replaced (KSÍ, 22 September; fotbolti.net). That leaves 23 players, and all 23 are in tonight’s matchday squad (Sekundomer). Players not in the squad made 48 of Iceland’s 154 competitive starts since September 2024. The most-used is Sverrir Ingi Ingason (12 starts), who ruptured a cruciate ligament in the summer and is out for the year. He is followed by Jóhann Berg Guðmundsson (7, without a club and considering retirement), Elías Rafn (6), Valgeir Lunddal Friðriksson (5) and Arnór Ingvi Traustason (4). Hjörtur Hermannsson and Kristian Hlynsson are injured (fotbolti.net). Players in the squad scored 21 of the 23 goals that were not own goals: Orri Óskarsson 5, Albert Guðmundsson 4, Andri Lucas Guðjohnsen, Victor Pálsson and Ísak Bergmann Jóhannesson 3 each. Andri Lucas is back from injury. In goal, Hákon Rafn Valdimarsson (8 starts) starts, back from a back injury, with Rúnar Alex Rúnarsson recalled after a long absence (fotbolti.net). Tonight’s back four is Victor Pálsson, Stefán Teitur Þórðarson, Daníel Leó Grétarsson and Mikael Egill Ellertsson (9, 6, 9 and 8 starts in the window), with Stefán Teitur, listed as a midfielder, at centre-back (fotbolti.net). Aron Einar Gunnarsson, 37, made 2 and says this Nations League is his last competition for Iceland (fotbolti.net, 22 September). Ísak Snær Þorvaldsson (Lyngby) returns.
  • Estonia — coach Jürgen Henn, in charge since June 2024; turnover medium in the squad, high in attack. Henn was announced on 15 May 2024 and took over for the 2024 Baltic Cup (Wikipedia), so all 14 competitive matches are his. He named 26 on 15 September (Estonian FA). Out on health grounds: Rauno Sappinen, Vlasiy Sinyavskiy, Vladislav Kreida, Kristo Hussar, Marten-Chris Paalberg, Tony Varjund and Nikita Mihhailov. Rocco Robert Shein declined at his own wish, and Soufian Gouram asked to stay with his club. Henri Anier (104 caps, 23 goals) was injured on 19 September and replaced by centre-back Joonas Tamm, 34, who last played for his club in March (Estonian FA; Soccernet). Players not in the squad made 41 of Estonia’s 154 competitive starts: Sinyavskiy 13, Shein 9, Sappinen 7, Ioan Yakovlev 7 (not called; no reason found), Anier 2, Martin Vetkal 2 (Henn, in a Soccernet headline: the door is not closed, but “one trust issue” is unresolved) and Michael Lilander 1. They scored 5 of Estonia’s 11 competitive goals (Sappinen 2, Sinyavskiy, Shein and Yakovlev one each); Mattias Käit (3), Paskotši, Peetson and Saarma scored the rest. Karl Hein kept goal in 13 of 14, and the back line is largely intact: Schjønning-Larsen 13 starts, Paskotši 11, Mets 8, Kuusk 7, Joonas Tamm 5. Up front, the matchday squad leaves out Alex Matthias Tamm (6 starts, no goal), whom Sports Mole’s preview had leading the line, and Sander Alamaa and Tanel Tammik (Sekundomer). That leaves two forwards, Karel Mustmaa (5 caps, 1 goal) and Mattias Männilaan (3 caps), with no competitive start between them in the window. ESPN’s bench lists Alex Matthias Tamm; see Elevens. Henn names Saarma, Skvortsov and Ainsalu as other options further forward, and says Joonas Tamm may be tried in attack (Soccernet).
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side in any source read, and Henn says every Estonian player was fit at the last training (Soccernet). Aron Einar Gunnarsson’s last competitive appearance ended in a second-yellow dismissal (69th minute, Murcia, 23 March 2025), and he has played only friendlies since. B · Style & early-season reality

Iceland’s shape moved with the coach. In the 2024–25 League B group, before Arnar, they played a 4-4-2 in four of six, had 40.5% of the ball and were outshot 12.17 to 15.83. Under Arnar they have had 53.0% of the ball over eight, in five different shapes: a 4-4-2 three times, a 5-3-2 in both France matches, a 3-4-3 in the 5–0 against Azerbaijan, a 4-2-3-1 in Baku and a 4-4-1-1 in Pristina. ESPN lists tonight’s as a 4-3-3; fotbolti.net describes two forwards with Albert and Jón Dagur wide. Against Montenegro and Azerbaijan they had 55.6%, took 13.75 shots to 8.75 and put in 19.8 crosses per match, 25.5 in the two at home. Arnar’s stated plan for tonight is “clearly more of the ball … first and foremost a lot of crosses and noise in the box” (fotbolti.net, 25 September); that is a statement, not a match. Their competitive matches have been open: over 2.5 in 10 of 14 and 5 of 6 at home. They conceded late, 9 of 29 goals from the 76th minute. They foul more at home than away (14.00 against 9.86) and are rarely booked for it, one card per 8.40 fouls. The summer changes are at the back. The goalkeeper who started both 2025 clean sheets is out, and Hákon Rafn, who starts, is back from a back injury. Sverrir Ingi, who started three of the four matches against C-or-below sides, is out for the year. Tonight’s back four made 9, 6, 9 and 8 starts in the window, and one of its centre-backs, Stefán Teitur, is listed as a midfielder.

Estonia play without the ball. Over 14 competitive matches they had 37.7% of the ball and were outshot 6.86 to 22.21. They won 2.00 corners to 8.00, and did not win the corner count in any of the 14. In their own 2024–25 League C group those figures were 33.8%, 5.83 shots to 22.50 and 1.83 corners to 9.00. Against Slovakia, Azerbaijan and Moldova the gap narrowed to 43.6%, 8.83 shots to 18.67 and 2.33 corners to 6.83, and they led at half-time in three of six. Henn mostly sets up in a 4-2-3-1 (nine of 14; 4-4-1-1 four times, 5-4-1 in Oslo). They foul much more than they are fouled (13.79 against 8.79) at one card per 6.03 fouls, and all three of their red cards came away. They were level at half-time in 8 of 14, six of them 0–0, and conceded 20 of their 30 goals after the break. The summer removes most of the attack that scored their 11. The five goals of Sappinen, Sinyavskiy, Shein and Yakovlev are gone, and so is Anier. Tonight Henn starts with five at the back, a shape he used once in the 14 (1–4 in Oslo), and Mustmaa, with no competitive start in the window, alone up front. Henn expects Iceland to “want to dominate” and says “if we don’t have the ball ourselves, it will be hard” (Soccernet). The evidence for Estonia’s replacement front line is a name list, not a match.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — the two team records agree at this level; the division prior does not
Iceland won all four competitive matches against sides now in League C or below (11–0), two of them at home; Estonia won one of seven away and lost all eight against League A and B sides. Against C-or-below sides Estonia went W2 D2 L2, losing only to Slovakia, twice, by one goal each time. The pairing prior is flat: relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors went W6 D5 L6 (17). What keeps this at OK: Iceland’s four wins came against two opponents, the centre-back who started three of them is out, and a player listed as a midfielder starts at centre-back.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — the two margin records point at different sizes
Iceland’s four margins against C-or-below sides were +2, +2, +2 and +5; Estonia’s six against such sides ran from −1 to +2, and none of their defeats at that level was by more than one. Estonia lost by three or more only to Sweden, Italy and Norway. Relegated-from-B hosts won by two or more in 4 of 17, lost by two or more in 2, and finished within one goal in 11. Neither side’s back line or attack tonight is the one that set its margins.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — split: both team records at this level sit under the division line, the pairing prior above it
Over 2.5 came in 1 of Iceland’s 4 matches against C-or-below sides and 2 of Estonia’s 6, with under 1.5 in 3 of Estonia’s, against League C’s 47.9%. The headline rates point the other way, but they were set against League A and B sides: 71.4% and 64.3% over 14, 83.3% at Laugardalsvöllur. Relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors ran 52.9% (17). Estonia’s shot volume against C-or-below sides (8.83) and the changes at Iceland’s goalkeeper and centre-back are the parts no window has tested.
BTTSOK — Estonia’s scoring half is steady; Iceland’s conceding half turned on opposition and now on a new keeper and back line
Estonia failed to score in 7 of 14 competitive matches, 4 of 7 away and 5 of 6 in their 2024–25 League C group, on 2.43 shots on target per match. The scorers of 5 of their 11 goals are out. Iceland conceded in none of their four against C-or-below sides and in all ten against League A and B sides. BTTS ran 0.0% and 50.0% against C-or-below sides, against League C’s 37.5%; relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors ran 58.8% (17).
Team goalsSplit — Estonia side OK, best on the card; Iceland side OK

Players out of the squad scored 5 of their 11 goals; tonight’s eleven scored the other 5 (Käit 3) and is led by Mustmaa, with no competitive start, in a 5-4-1. Their best window, 1.17 per match against C-or-below sides, still includes three matches without a goal. Tonight’s eleven scored 20 of their 23 goals that were not own goals, 9 of them Orri Óskarsson’s and Albert Guðmundsson’s.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK at this level; 1H result dangerous
Against C-or-below sides both first-half lines ran 1.00 goals per match (Iceland 4–0 in four, Estonia 5–1 in six), against League C’s 1.15, and Estonia were level at the break in 8 of 14 (six at 0–0). The half-time result points two ways. Iceland were never level at half-time at home (led 4, trailed 2), and Estonia were level in five of seven away. Relegated-from-B hosts led at half-time in 4 of 17 and trailed in 7. Estonia conceded 20 of 30 after the break, which moves their exposure out of the first half.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — asymmetric: every line books the visitor more; the match total and the official’s two records straddle the division
Iceland’s home matches ran 5.00 cards (Iceland 1.67, visitors 3.33); Estonia’s away matches 3.86 (Estonia 2.57, hosts 1.29); relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors 4.76 (host 1.88, visitor 2.88). All three put the visitor above the host, and Estonia commit 13.79 fouls per match to 8.79 suffered. The total is less settled. League C runs 4.85, and Milanović books 3.50 per match at 6.65 fouls per card in ESPN’s UEFA and international record, but 5.00 at about 5.4 in the Serbian league. His one senior international, in League D, had eight cards.
CornersOK — direction consistent at this level, volume not
Estonia did not win the corner count in any of their 14 competitive matches, including all six against C-or-below sides (2.33 won, 6.83 conceded). Relegated-from-B hosts won it in 12 of 17 against stayed-in-C visitors (6.29 to 2.71). Iceland’s own volume is the weak part: 3.67 won per match at home and 4.00 against C-or-below sides, against League C’s 4.58. Arnar’s stated plan is crosses, and his side put in 25.5 per match at home against C-or-below sides.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both headline records were set mostly a level up. Ten of Iceland’s 14 competitive matches and 8 of Estonia’s 14 were against sides now in Leagues A and B. At this level, Iceland’s evidence is four matches against two opponents, and Estonia’s is six against three. Every rate that moves between those windows has to be read from the small one: totals, BTTS, clean sheets, possession, shots.
  • The personnel changes sit where the small windows were built. Sverrir Ingi started three of Iceland’s four matches against C-or-below sides, and Elías Rafn kept goal in two; both are out, and Stefán Teitur, listed as a midfielder, starts at centre-back. Estonia’s attack at that level ran through Sappinen, Sinyavskiy, Shein, Yakovlev and Anier, none of whom is in the squad. Tonight Mustmaa leads it alone in a back-five shape Estonia used once in 14.
  • Long gaps, and friendlies that point opposite ways. Iceland last played competitively 314 days ago and Estonia 317. Since then Iceland went winless in five friendlies against Mexico, Canada, Haiti, Japan and Argentina (3–11); Estonia went W3 D1 L1 against Cyprus, Kenya, Rwanda, the Faroe Islands and Lithuania. Iceland’s part of the window was under a coach who has since changed shape almost match by match.
  • The official’s record is mostly club and youth football. Milanović has one senior international in ESPN’s record and none above League D, and his UEFA and domestic card rates differ by 1.5 per match.
  • Most sensitive: handicap and the match card total. The first rests on Iceland’s margins against two opponents with a changed defence; the second on an official whose two records straddle the division. Least sensitive: Estonia’s team goals and the corner direction, where the League C group, the away record, the qualifiers and the absences all point the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Estonia team goals are more robust than Iceland team goals, because Estonia failed to score in 7 of 14 and in 5 of 6 in their own League C group, on 2.43 shots on target per match. The scorers of 5 of their 11 goals are out, and tonight Mustmaa, with no competitive start, leads the line alone. Iceland’s forward line is intact (Orri, Albert, Andri Lucas and Jón Dagur, 13 goals), but ESPN lists tonight’s shape as a 4-3-3, which it did not record for them in the 14, and it faces five defenders.
  • Corner direction is more robust than corner volume, because Estonia were out-cornered or level in all 14 competitive matches and relegated-from-B hosts won the count in 12 of 17. The volume needs Iceland’s own count, which ran below League C’s 4.58 at home.
  • The card split is more robust than the card total, because every line reached books the visitor more than the host: 3.33 against 1.67 in Iceland’s home matches, 2.57 against 1.29 in Estonia’s away matches, 2.88 against 1.88 in the pairing prior. The total turns on whether Milanović referees like his UEFA record (3.50) or his Serbian one (5.00).
  • First-half goals are more robust than the first-half result, because both sides ran 1.00 first-half goals per match against C-or-below sides and Estonia were level at half-time in 8 of 14. Iceland’s home half-times were never level, which splits the result.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is the official’s card rate. Milanović’s UEFA and international record in ESPN (3.50 cards per match, 6.65 fouls per card) and his Serbian league record on playerstats.football (5.00, about 5.4) sit on either side of League C’s 4.85 and 5.32. That decides whether Discipline reads on the total or only on the split. A published Serbian record with fouls per card and penalties by season, from the Serbian federation or a statistics site that tabulates them, would settle which record to set against the division.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Czechia are promoted: League B Group B1 winners in 2024–25 on 11 points, 9–8 (W3 D2 L1, ahead of Ukraine, Georgia and Albania). Their division history reads B/A/B/A. Their only previous League A edition, 2022–23, ended fourth in Group A2 on 4 points, 5–13 (W1 D1 L4), and they went down; at home in Prague they beat Switzerland 2–1, drew 2–2 with Spain and lost 0–4 to Portugal. Sports Mole puts their overall Nations League record at W10 D3 L9 in 22. In World Cup qualifying they were second in Group L on 16 points, 18–8 (W5 D1 L2), behind Croatia. They reached the World Cup through two home play-offs, both drawn after extra time and won on penalties: 2–2 with the Republic of Ireland and 2–2 with Denmark (1–1 at 90 minutes). At the World Cup they finished bottom of Group A on 1 point: 1–2 to South Korea in Guadalajara, 1–1 with South Africa in Atlanta, 0–3 to Mexico in Mexico City. Croatia have played in League A in every edition. In 2024–25 they were second in Group A1 on 8 points, 8–8 (W2 D2 L2, behind Portugal), then lost the quarter-final to France on penalties after 2–0 in Split and 0–2 in Saint-Denis. They won qualifying Group L on 22 points, 26–4 (W7 D1), including the 5–1 against Czechia in Osijek and the 0–0 in Prague. At the World Cup they were second in Group L (2–4 to England, 1–0 against Panama, 2–1 against Ghana) and lost 1–2 to Portugal in the round of 32. The structural headline: both coaches take their first match, and the home side’s squad has changed far more than the visitor’s. Players in tonight’s Czech squad made 104 of Czechia’s 209 competitive starts since September 2024 and scored 16 of their 32 goals. For Croatia the figures are 192 of 220 starts and 37 of their 40 goals that were not own goals. The division prior for this pairing is thin. In the 24 League A matches of 2022–23 and 2024–25 in which a side just promoted from League B was at home, the home side went W5 D8 L11, scored 0.88 and conceded 1.42 per match, and 18 of the 24 finished within one goal (baselines_0926.md, added on this card). Head to head: six meetings in the match record, Croatia W2 D4, 13–7: 1–1 in Casablanca (1996), 4–2 in Pula (2011, friendly), 2–2 at Euro 2016, 1–1 at Euro 2020, 5–1 in Osijek and 0–0 in Prague (both 2025). ESPN’s last five agree (Croatia W2 D3). Sports Mole gives Czechia “three draws and three defeats” in six; not reconciled. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for all of both sides’ competitive matches (19 and 20), set against the squads as Wikipedia lists them.

  • Czechia — coach Santi Denia, appointed 31 July 2026; turnover high, in midfield, at centre-forward and across the back line. Denia, 52, replaced Miroslav Koubek, who resigned after the World Cup. He signed for two years with an option for two more, and led Spain to the 2024 Olympic gold (AP via Yahoo Sports and France 24, search summary). He is the first foreign coach of the national team. Koubek had been appointed on 19 December 2025, after Hašek was sacked. Players not in tonight’s squad made 105 of the 209 starts. The most-used are Tomáš Souček (15, not in the squad), Lukáš Provod (12, injured), Patrik Schick (11, retired from international football after the World Cup), and Jaroslav Zelený, Václav Černý and Tomáš Holeš (10 each, not in the squad). Tomáš Chorý (8) is injured. Six withdrew injured at the start of the camp: Zima, Vlček, Jurásek, Provod, Višinský and Chorý. A seventh, Matěj Ryneš, tore ankle ligaments in training, which leaves one left-back (ČeskéNoviny, Tiscali headlines; Wikipedia marks all seven as injury withdrawals). In: Martin Vitík (Bologna), Václav Sejk and Ondřej Zmrzlý, and first call-ups Ondřej Kričfaluši (Union Saint-Gilloise), Martin Šubert, Jiří Sláma and Daniel Vašulín. Denia’s own list had already added Lukáš Ambros, Roman Macek and Matěj Radosta (uncapped) and brought back Alex Král and Michal Sáček. Scorers out of the squad: Schick 5, Souček 3, Chorý 3, Černý 2 and three others with one each — 16 of the 32. Still in: Šulc (5), Hložek, Červ, Karabec and Krejčí (2 each), and goalkeeper Matěj Kovář, who made 18 of the 19 starts in goal.
  • Croatia — coach Slaven Bilić, appointed 13 July 2026; squad turnover low, bench changed. Bilić, 57, succeeded Zlatko Dalić, who stepped down after the World Cup. The federation’s executive board approved him unanimously (HNS, search summary). He coached Croatia from July 2006 to May 2012 (Sports Mole) and returns after two years without a job; his staff includes his nephew and former assistants (tportal headline). He named 26 on 7 September, and Lovro Majer was added later. Out: Josip Juranović and Martin Erlić, injured; Marin Pongračić and Kristijan Jakić came in (HNS, HRT, index.hr). Players not in the squad made 28 of the 220 starts, most of them Duje Ćaleta-Car’s (12, not in the squad) and Borna Sosa’s (6). Players in the squad scored 37 of the 40 non-own goals, led by Kramarić (7), Perišić (6) and Budimir (4). Dion Drena Beljo, who scored 38 for Dinamo last season, is new; he was not in Dalić’s World Cup squad. Modrić is 41 and still captain.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension reported for either side in any source read. Czechia: Schick retired; Zima, Vlček, Jurásek, Ryneš, Provod, Višinský and Chorý injured; Souček, Zelený, Černý and Holeš not in the squad. Croatia: Juranović and Erlić injured. Modrić and Baturina ran a temperature this week; Modrić trained on Friday and is expected to start, while Baturina is expected on the bench (index.hr, search summary). One search summary said both squads were fully fit. The withdrawals above contradict it, and it is not used. Both play four matches in 11 days. Czechia host England here on 29 September, then play Spain in Oviedo (3 October) and England at Wembley (6 October). Croatia play Spain in Seville (29 September), then host England in Rijeka (3 October) and Spain in Split (6 October) (Wikipedia’s League A page).

B · Style & early-season reality

Czechia’s shape changed with each coach, and none of the three is in charge tonight. Hašek mostly played a 4-2-3-1 (eight of his 13 matches in ESPN’s record). In qualifying (seven matches under Hašek, one under Köstl) Czechia had more of the ball than the opposition: 59.0%, 17.75 shots to 8.50, 7.75 corners to 2.62. His side also won more tackles (160), made more interceptions (88) and won more aerial duels (238) than any other UEFA qualifier, and scored eight set-piece goals, the most of any European side (Squawka, via search summary). Koubek used a back three in all five of his matches (3-4-3, 3-4-2-1 three times, 3-5-2) and had 23.0% to 57.8% of the ball. Against League A-tier sides Czechia had 40.5% (two box scores). Czechia foul a lot and are rarely booked for it: 15.00 fouls per match at home, one card for every 8.08 fouls. Their cards fell from 3.33 per match in the 2024–25 Nations League to 0.88 in qualifying. They conceded 5 of their 25 competitive goals before half-time and 20 after it. Sports Mole’s possible eleven keeps a back three. Denia’s record is with Spain’s Olympic side (gold in 2024), so the evidence for his shape is a CV, not a match.

Croatia played a 4-2-3-1 in 13 of 20 competitive matches under Dalić, with a back three in four of the six 2024–25 Nations League group matches (and a 5-4-1 in another), at home to the Faroe Islands and against England at the World Cup. They dominated weaker sides: 70.0% possession, 22.62 shots and 9.25 corners per match in qualifying. Against League A-tier opposition they had 48.3% of the ball, took 12.43 shots to 15.00 and won 4.00 corners to 7.14. The two meetings split the same way: Croatia had 61.4% and 57.7% of the ball, but won 0 corners to 6 in Osijek. Away from home their goals came early, six of eight before half-time, and they failed to score in 3 of 7. Their away discipline was loose: 2.57 cards per match on 9.00 fouls, with both of the window’s red cards. The personnel is continuous: Livaković, Šutalo, Modrić, Perišić and Gvardiol made 14 to 18 starts each, and all are in the squad. What changes is the bench. Bilić has no match in this spell, and his previous spell ended in 2012; any claim about his shape tonight is a CV, not a match.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — two coaches with no match, and a home side missing half its starters
Czechia are unbeaten at home in nine competitive matches (W6 D3), but drew both against League A-tier opposition (0–0 Croatia, 1–1 Denmark at 90 minutes). Croatia won two of seven away. Sides promoted from League B went W5 D8 L11 at home in League A (24). The four meetings since 2016 were three draws and Croatia’s 5–1 in Osijek. Every one of these figures was set under a different coach. Players out of tonight’s Czech squad made half of Czechia’s starts and scored half of their goals.
Asian handicap / DNBOK — narrow margins are what every window reached agrees on
Six of Croatia’s seven away matches finished within one goal; the exception was 0–2 in Saint-Denis. Czechia’s two home matches against League A-tier sides were draws. Among home sides just promoted into League A, 18 of 24 finished within one goal, with one home win by two or more (24). League A 2024–25 had 30 of 48 within one. The one wide margin in the pairing, 5–1 in Osijek, came at Croatia’s ground with two penalties.

Croatia’s away cards (2.57) include six in the Saint-Denis quarter-final and both red cards.
CornersDangerous — the corner split turns on the opposition’s level
Croatia won more corners in 5 of 6 away matches (7.17 to 4.50), but only 2 of 7 against League A-tier sides (4.00 to 7.14). Czechia won 6.43 to 4.43 at home, and 11 to 6 across the two meetings despite 38.6% and 42.3% of the ball. Promoted-from-B hosts conceded more (3.79 to 5.46; 24). Czechia’s corner volume belonged to Hašek’s possession side and Koubek’s back three, and Denia’s shape is unseen.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both coaches are new, and neither has a match. Czechia’s figures span three coaches (Hašek, Köstl, Koubek) and two shapes, a back four and a back three. Croatia’s come from Dalić alone. Denia’s evidence is an Olympic CV, and Bilić’s is a spell that ended in 2012.
  • Czechia’s home record was built mostly below this level. Seven of their nine home matches came against sides outside League A. Against the two League A-tier visitors they drew 0–0 and 1–1, and their previous League A campaign produced one win in six.
  • The personnel changes sit on one side. Players in tonight’s Czech squad made 104 of 209 starts and scored 16 of 32 goals; Croatia’s made 192 of 220 and scored 37 of 40. Czechia are down to one left-back.
  • Czechia’s discipline figures moved with the coach, not the opposition. They were booked 20 times in six 2024–25 Nations League matches, 7 in eight qualifiers and 3 in Koubek’s five, on similar foul counts. Any team-card reading tonight rests on a fourth coach.
  • Most sensitive: Czechia team goals and corners — the first loses half its scorers, the second changed with each Czech shape and with Croatia’s opposition level. Least sensitive: the margin reading and the match-card line, where Croatia’s away record, Czechia’s home draws against League A-tier sides, the promoted-host prior and the official all point the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Handicap is more robust than 1X2 here, because 6 of Croatia’s 7 away matches and 18 of 24 promoted-host matches in League A finished within one goal, while the direction of the result rests on two coaches with no match.
  • Match cards are more robust than either side’s cards, because every match-level line sits under League A’s 4.13 (Czechia at home 3.78, Croatia away 3.57, promoted hosts 3.67, Sozza 3.87). Czechia’s own count swung from 3.33 per match to 0.88 between two competitions.
  • Croatia team goals are more robust than Czechia team goals, because players in tonight’s Croatia squad scored 37 of their 40 non-own goals. Czechia’s squad scored 16 of 32, and Czechia scored 0.67 per match against League A-tier sides.
  • The total is more robust than BTTS, because the away and pairing lines agree (Croatia away 42.9%, promoted hosts 41.7%, against League A’s 56.3%). BTTS needs Czechia’s halved scoring side and Croatia’s away scoring, which failed in 3 of 7.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is Czechia’s eleven and shape under Denia: back three or back four, and who leads the line without Schick and Chorý. It settles when the elevens are published before kickoff. The shape moves corners and team goals most. Croatia’s back four — Sports Mole’s Šutalo and Pongračić, or index.hr’s Vušković and Perišić — is the second fact.

England – Spain

20:45 CEST · Wembley Stadium, London · England are promoted as League B Group B2 winners and back in League A after one edition away. Their previous League A edition (2022–23) ended fourth without a win, and they went down; since then they have reached a World Cup semi-final and taken bronze. Spain stayed in League A and are world and European champions, with no competitive defeat at 90 minutes in the 24 matches since September 2024. Neither coach has changed: Tuchel’s contract runs to Euro 2028, and de la Fuente’s was renewed on 21 September to 2032. A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

England are promoted: League B Group B2 winners in 2024–25, W5 L1, 16–3, all six matches under interim coach Lee Carsley. The one defeat was 1–2 to Greece at Wembley. Their division history, 2018–19 to 2026–27, reads A/A/A/B/A. Their previous League A edition, 2022–23, ended fourth in Group A3 with no win: W0 D3 L3, 4–10. At home that season they drew 0–0 with Italy and lost 0–4 to Hungary at Molineux, then drew 3–3 with Germany at Wembley. Under Tuchel they won qualifying Group K with eight wins, 22–0. At the World Cup they won Group L (4–2 Croatia, 0–0 Ghana, 2–0 Panama). They then beat Congo DR 2–1, Mexico 3–2 in Mexico City and Norway 2–1 after extra time. They lost the semi-final 1–2 to Argentina after leading until the 85th minute, and beat France 6–4 for third place. Spain have played in League A in every edition. In 2024–25 they won Group A4, W5 D1, 13–4. They beat the Netherlands on penalties in the quarter-final (2–2 and 3–3 after extra time) and France 5–4 in the semi-final, then lost the final to Portugal on penalties after 2–2. They won the 2022–23 edition. They won qualifying Group E, W5 D1, 21–2. At the World Cup they were unbeaten in eight and conceded one goal: 0–0 Cape Verde, 4–0 Saudi Arabia, 1–0 Uruguay, 3–0 Austria, 1–0 Portugal, 2–1 Belgium, 2–0 France. The final with Argentina was 0–0 at 90 minutes and won 1–0 in extra time. SI and Flashscore count 38 matches without a 90-minute defeat since a 1–0 friendly loss to Colombia in March 2024. ESPN’s competitive window agrees for its 24 matches. The structural headline: the promoted side is a World Cup semi-finalist, and the personnel changes are on the home side. Players in tonight’s England squad made 144 of England’s 242 competitive starts since September 2024 (112 of 176 under Tuchel) and scored 40 of their 55 goals that were not own goals. For Spain’s matchday 23 the figures are 214 of 264 starts and 51 of 58. The division prior fits England loosely. In the 24 League A matches of 2022–23 and 2024–25 in which a side just promoted from League B was at home, the home side went W5 D8 L11, scoring 0.88 and conceding 1.42 per match (baselines_0926.md). None of the eight sides in that line had come down after one edition, as England did. Head to head: 28 meetings in the match record, England W13 D4 L11, 46–34, counting the Euro 96 quarter-final (0–0, won by England on penalties) as a draw. Sports Mole and VAVEL give England 14 wins, which matches the record only if that shootout counts as a win; Excelsior gives 13, 11 and 4. The last five: 0–2 in Alicante (2015, friendly), 2–2 at Wembley (2016, friendly), 1–2 at Wembley and 3–2 in Seville (2018 Nations League), 1–2 in the Euro 2024 final in Berlin. SI and Flashscore say Spain have won six of the last nine. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for both sides’ competitive matches (22 and 24), set against the squads as England Football and Wikipedia list them.

Turnover high, in central midfield, at full-back and at centre-back. Goal.com reported a World Cup-related break clause in the extension (search summary). Tuchel was heavily criticised for the late stages of the Argentina semi-final (Sports Mole, SI, Sky Sports), but he stays in charge. Out: Pedro Porro (16 starts; injured with Tottenham, replaced by Iván Fresneda), Marcos Llorente (retired from international football after the World Cup), goalkeeper Joan García (knee) and Borja Iglesias (injured). B · Style & early-season reality

Tuchel’s England played a 4-2-3-1 in 12 of his 16 competitive matches (4-3-3 twice, 4-1-4-1 twice). Their possession fell with the opposition’s level. They had 74.4% in qualifying, where they took 18.88 shots to 4.25 and 7.50 corners to 2.12 and conceded 2.1 xG in eight. That rose to 54.3% at the World Cup and fell to 45.7% in the knockout rounds. They had 35.8% against Argentina (5 shots to 15), and 33.1% against Mexico after Quansah’s 54th-minute red card. After Gordon scored against Argentina, England made 26 passes and had 11.9% of the ball until the winner (Opta via Flashscore). After the World Cup Tuchel said English players cannot control games with possession the way Spain’s can, and spoke of having to “embrace the chaos” with more direct play (Sky Sports). Under Tuchel England commit fewer fouls than they suffer (9.93 against 12.60) and are rarely booked (0.94 cards per match). Across the window they crossed far more than their opponents (21.2 per match to 11.2), and 61% of their goals (35 of 57) came after half-time. This summer the change is in personnel, not in the coach: Rice, the most-used starter after Pickford and Kane, is out. So are three of the semi-final back four (Stones, James, Spence), and the right-back choice is between a centre-back and a recalled Alexander-Arnold. Any claim about the shape tonight rests on a press conference and previews, not a match.

Spain’s shape is settled and their personnel continuous. They used a 4-3-3 in 11 of 24 and a 4-2-3-1 in 13, including every World Cup match from the last group game onwards. They hold the ball against everyone: 62.9% across the window, 59.6% against League A-tier sides and 63.8% at the World Cup. At the World Cup they allowed 6.29 shots and 1.43 on target per match, conceded one goal and 2.4 xG in eight (FotMob’s lowest), and Unai Simón set a record of six consecutive World Cup clean sheets (Wikipedia, citing Marca). Their attacking volume dropped there, from 22.83 shots and 3.50 goals per match in qualifying to 17.14 and 1.62. Four of the eight finished under 1.5 goals. Against League A-tier sides their margins were narrow: 10 of 13 finished within one goal. Opponents are booked more than Spain (2.25 per match against 1.29), and against League A-tier sides 2.85 against 1.62. Spain commit 11.10 fouls per match, more than England. Corners run 6.29 to 3.14 in their favour overall, and 5.18 to 4.09 against League A-tier sides. In the three competitive meetings Spain had 54.8%, 73.1% and 65.1% of the ball and won 23 corners to 6. The only structural change is at right-back, where the man six previews name has no competitive start in the window.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — two records built mostly against lesser opposition, and the home squad has changed
England are W6 L1 at home (7), but only two of those matches came against League A-tier sides (1–2 Greece, 2–0 Serbia). Spain have not lost at 90 minutes in 24 competitive matches, with 5 draws in 13 against League A-tier sides. Promoted hosts went W5 D8 L11 in League A (24), but England are unlike the sides in that line. Spain have won six of the last nine meetings (SI, Flashscore); the full record is England W13 D4 L11. Players out of tonight’s England squad made 98 of England’s 242 starts.
Asian handicap / DNBOK — narrow margins are what the top-opposition windows agree on
Ten of Spain’s 13 matches against League A-tier sides finished within one goal, as did four of England’s five World Cup knockout matches at 90 minutes. Four of the last five meetings did too; the exception was 2–0 in Alicante in 2015. Promoted hosts in League A finished within one goal in 18 of 24, and League A 2024–25 in 30 of 48. The exception is England against League A-tier sides, 2 of 7, where the wide margins came against Serbia, Greece and France in the third-place match.
Totals (O/U goals)Dangerous — the windows run from 1.75 to 4.43 goals per match
Spain’s World Cup produced 1.75 goals per match, with under 1.5 in 4 of 8; England against League A-tier sides produced 4.43. England’s World Cup produced 3.88, with England conceding in every knockout match. Over 2.5 came in 42.9% of England’s home matches and 41.7% of promoted-host matches, against League A’s 56.3%. It came in four of the last five meetings. Only two of England’s home matches were against League A-tier opposition.
BTTSDangerous — the World Cup records point opposite ways
England conceded in all five World Cup knockout matches; Spain conceded once in eight World Cup matches. BTTS ran 12.5% for Spain at the World Cup and 14.3% for England at home. It ran 57.1% and 53.8% against League A-tier sides, 54.2% for promoted hosts (24) and 58.3% in League A, and came in four of the last five meetings. Stones, James, Spence and Rice, who made 40 of England’s starts, are all out.
Team goalsSplit — Spain side OK, best on the card; England side dangerous
Spain’s side rests on a squad that made 81% of their starts and scored 51 of their 58 non-own goals. Spain scored in 21 of 24 and in 12 of 13 against League A-tier sides, and England’s changed back line conceded 10 in five World Cup knockout matches. England’s side has its own record: they scored in 21 of 22 and in all 7 against League A-tier sides, and Kane scored 17 of their 55. But it meets the World Cup’s lowest xG conceded (2.4 in eight) and 6.29 shots allowed per match. England are also without Rice, Rashford and Palmer.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Dangerous — each side’s first-half profile changes between windows
England scored 13 of their 17 home goals after half-time and were level at 0–0 at the break in 3 of 7, but against League A-tier sides their first halves ran 1.57/0.43. Spain scored 30 of 58 before half-time overall, but only 3 of 8 in the World Cup knockout rounds, and they were level at the break in 4 of 8 World Cup matches. Promoted hosts averaged 1.00 first-half goals per match (24), against League A’s 1.33. The three competitive meetings stood 1–2, 3–0 and 0–0 at half-time (England first).
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — the official sits on League A’s line; the team split is pulled two ways
Massa averages 4.12 cards per match (64) and 4.14 in internationals (7), against League A’s 4.13; his fouls per card is 5.86 against 5.52. His red-card rate is 0.30 per match against 0.21. The match lines straddle his: England against League A-tier sides 2.14, Spain 4.46, promoted hosts 3.67, the three meetings 5, 7 and 4. The team split is the unstable part. England were booked 0.94 times per match under Tuchel, while Spain’s opponents are booked 2.25 times per match to Spain’s 1.29, and those two tendencies pull the split in opposite directions.
CornersOK — the total agrees across windows; the split turns on who has the ball
Match corner totals sit between 8.43 and 10.67 per match in every team window, 9.25 for promoted hosts, against League A’s 9.50. The split follows possession. England won more in 7 of 7 at home with 73.7% of the ball, but 3.25 to 6.00 in the World Cup knockout rounds with 45.7%. Spain won 23 to 6 across the three competitive meetings, although the 2018 Wembley match went 4 to 1 to England. The meeting totals were 5, 12 and 12.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • The promotion label misreads England. They came up from a League B season under an interim coach, after one relegation from a winless League A edition, and have since reached a World Cup semi-final. The promoted-host prior (W5 D8 L11) was built on Austria, Czechia, Hungary, Wales, Scotland, Israel, Bosnia-Herzegovina and Serbia.
  • Both records were built largely below this level. Fifteen of England’s 22 matches and 11 of Spain’s 24 came against sides outside League A. England’s seven home matches include two against League A-tier sides, and Spain’s World Cup mixes Cape Verde and Saudi Arabia with France and Argentina.
  • This is both sides’ first match in 69–70 days, with no friendly in between. It is England’s first since a semi-final whose late stages brought heavy criticism of the coach (Sports Mole, SI, Sky Sports), and Spain’s first as world champions.
  • The personnel changes sit on one side. Tonight’s England squad made 144 of 242 starts; four of the semi-final eleven are out, and the right-back and Rice’s place in midfield are open. Spain’s 23 made 214 of 264, and ten of the final eleven are in it.
  • Most sensitive: totals, BTTS and England team goals. England’s scoring and conceding records meet the World Cup’s best defence and a Spain attack that slowed there, with a changed England back line and midfield. Least sensitive: the margin reading and the corner total, where every window reached lands in the same band.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Handicap is more robust than 1X2 here, because 10 of Spain’s 13 matches against League A-tier sides, 4 of England’s 5 World Cup knockout matches and 4 of the last 5 meetings finished within one goal. The direction of the result rests on two records built mostly against weaker opposition.
  • The corner total is more robust than the corner split, because every team window lands between 8.43 and 10.67 per match (League A 9.50). The split swung from England 8.14 to 2.14 at home to 3.25 to 6.00 in the World Cup knockouts, and Spain won the meetings’ corners 23 to 6.
  • Spain team goals are more robust than England team goals, because Spain’s 23 scored 51 of their 58 non-own goals, and England’s back line and holding midfielder lose Stones, James, Spence and Rice (40 starts). England’s scoring side meets a defence that conceded 2.4 xG in eight World Cup matches.
  • Match cards are more robust than either side’s cards, because Massa (4.12) sits on League A’s 4.13. England’s low booking rate under Tuchel (0.94) and Spain’s habit of drawing cards from opponents (2.25 against 1.29) pull the split in opposite directions.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is England’s eleven and how deep it sits: who partners Anderson without Rice, and whether Quansah or Alexander-Arnold plays right-back. It settles when the elevens are published before kickoff. The shape moves corners, BTTS and team goals most. Spain’s right-back (Eric García, with no competitive start in the window) and whether Raya or Simón keeps goal is the second fact.

UEFA · Nations League B, Group B1 · Matchday 1

North Macedonia – Switzerland

20:45 CEST · Toše Proeski Arena, Skopje · The first meeting of the two sides. North Macedonia are promoted as League C Group C4 winners and play their first League B match; Goce Sedloski, appointed on 22 December 2025, takes his first match in Skopje after four away, W0 D2 L2 with no goal scored. Switzerland are relegated from League A for the first time, ten weeks after a World Cup quarter-final, with Murat Yakin still in charge. They are without Xhaka and Embolo (both out of the camp over Covid-certificate cases, Embolo also suspended), Zakaria, Amenda and Vargas (injured), and three World Cup players who have retired from the national team. A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

North Macedonia are promoted from League C: winners of Group C4 in 2024–25 on 16 points, 10–1 (W5 D1 L0, ahead of Armenia, the Faroe Islands and Latvia), and this is their first League B edition (League C in 2020–21, 2022–23 and 2024–25 in ESPN’s standings; “the first time we are in this competition”, Darko Pančev to the FFM’s magazine). In World Cup qualifying they were third in Group J on 13 points, 13–10 (W3 D4 L1, behind Belgium and Wales), reached the play-offs through the Nations League and lost the semi-final 0–4 in Copenhagen. Switzerland are relegated from League A: fourth in Group A4 in 2024–25 on 2 points, 6–14 (W0 D2 L4, behind Spain, Denmark and Serbia), their first relegation to League B (SRF). They then won qualifying Group B on 14 points, 14–2 (W4 D2 L0, ahead of Kosovo), won World Cup Group B on 7 points (W2 D1 L0 against Qatar, Bosnia-Herzegovina and Canada), beat Algeria 2–0 and Colombia on penalties after 0–0, and went out to Argentina 1–3 after extra time, 1–1 at 90 minutes, with Embolo sent off in the 72nd minute. The structural headline is that both records split hard by the level of the opponent, and tonight each side meets a level it has not faced in this form: North Macedonia went W8 D2 L0 (20–2) against League C and D sides and W0 D3 L2 (3–13) against League A sides, and have not played a League B side; Switzerland went W5 D2 L0 (18–3) against League B sides and W0 D2 L4 (6–14) against their League A group, and arrive without their captain, their first-choice centre-forward and three other World Cup regulars. The eight sides promoted from League C into League B for 2022–23 and 2024–25 took 1.30 points per home match over 23 (W8 D6 L9), scoring 1.09 and conceding 1.30, with over 2.5 in 39.1% and 11 of 23 goalless at half-time; none of the eight won its League B group. Hosting a side just relegated from League A they went W1 D3 L4, 6–15, and failed to score in five of eight. Head to head: none; the sides have never met (international results record; gol.mk; SRF). The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for the 15 competitive matches North Macedonia and the 18 Switzerland have played since September 2024, set against ESPN’s current squad lists and the two federations’ announcements as reported.

  • North Macedonia — coach change; turnover medium, concentrated in central defence and on the flanks of the attack. Blagoja Milevski’s tenure ended after the qualifying group (Wikipedia gives 2 September 2021 to 18 November 2025, his last match; the search summary of macedonianfootball.com’s coaches list gives 30 November 2025 and says his contract expired). The FFM’s board appointed Goce Sedloski at its Monday meeting, 22 December 2025 (macedonianfootball.com; the date from the search summary of its coaches list; Wikipedia’s coaches table gives 23 December, the day of his presentation). Sedloski, 52, won 100 caps as a player; as a coach he won the domestic league with Vardar in 2015–16 and 2016–17 and with Shkupi in 2021–22 and the cup with Vardar in 2024–25. His four matches: 0–4 in Denmark (play-off semi-final), 0–0 in Ireland, 0–0 in Bosnia-Herzegovina and 0–4 in Türkiye, all away, set up 4-2-3-1 in the play-off and with three centre-backs in all three friendlies (3-4-2-1, 3-4-3, 3-4-1-2). Milevski’s side lined up with a back four in 8 of his 14 competitive matches (4-2-3-1 in 4, 4-4-2 in 3, 4-1-4-1 in 1) and with three or five at the back in 6 (5-3-2 in 4, three of them in his last four qualifiers; 3-4-1-2 in 2). Sedloski named 28 on 16 September, four of them uncapped (Matej Gashtarov, 19, Crvena zvezda; Anes Meliqi, 20, Shkëndija; Gjorge Djekov, 20, Sileks; Hristijan Maleski, Struga) (makfudbal). Out: Nikola Serafimov (6 competitive starts, 1 goal) is injured (makfudbal); Darko Churlinov (8 starts, 1 goal) stayed in Poland “for administrative reasons” and Boban Nikolov was injured in training (ekipa.mk); Darko Velkovski (2 starts) was not called (gol.mk); Isnik Alimi (11 starts, 1 goal) and Lirim Qamili (1 start, 2 goals) have not been called since March; Aleksandar Trajkovski (5 starts, 2 goals) is marked retired from the national team on Wikipedia, with no date or statement found. Players outside tonight’s 26 made 48 of North Macedonia’s 165 competitive starts in the window and scored 7 of their 22 goals (a 23rd was an own goal). The core is intact: Stole Dimitrievski (15 of 15 starts) in goal, Enis Bardhi (15, 4 goals, the captain and the side’s one penalty-kick taker in the window), Bojan Miovski (14, 6 goals, the top scorer), Eljif Elmas (14, 1), Gjoko Zajkov (13), Ezgjan Alioski (11, 1) and Visar Musliu (9, 1). Nine of the eleven who started the last competitive match are in the squad.
  • Switzerland — no coach change; turnover high, through the middle of the pitch and the attack. Murat Yakin has been in charge since 9 August 2021 (Wikipedia). His July 2024 contract was 2+2 years, extending automatically to Euro 2028 on World Cup qualification (SRF), which followed. Out: Granit Xhaka (17 of 18 competitive starts, 3 goals, 3 of the 7 penalty kicks) admitted on 21 September to having bought a Covid certificate four years ago; he and the federation agreed he would miss this camp (SRF, watson), and he is to be questioned by the Lucerne public prosecutor in early October (watson). Breel Embolo (17 starts, 6 goals, the top scorer, 2 penalty kicks) was suspended for tonight after his red card against Argentina (SRF, Nau), and was then withdrawn from the whole camp after the Basel public prosecutor’s 2025 penalty order against him for repeated incitement to forge test certificates was reported by CH Media (a fine, over forged test certificates); the federation says the measure covers this camp, not November (bote.ch, CH Media). Denis Zakaria (5 starts) and Aurèle Amenda (1) withdrew injured; Isaac Schmidt, Vincent Sierro and Joël Monteiro were called up for them and Xhaka; Rubén Vargas (11 starts, 3 goals) is injured (SRF). Silvan Widmer (9 starts, 1 goal), Christian Fassnacht and Noah Okafor (1 start) have retired from the national team (SRF, Nau; Yakin criticised Okafor’s defensive work in reply to his statement). Players outside tonight’s 24 made 72 of Switzerland’s 198 competitive starts in the window and scored 14 of their 30 goals. In: first call-ups for Winsley Boteli (20, Sion; 12 goals this season, per SRF) and Sascha Britschgi (20, Parma); Zachary Athekame (21, Lyon) is also uncapped; Alvyn Sanches and Adrian Bajrami return (Nau, SRF). In the squad: Gregor Kobel (17 of 18), Ricardo Rodriguez (17), Manuel Akanji (16, 1), Remo Freuler (16, 1), Nico Elvedi (15, 1), Dan Ndoye (12, 4), Fabian Rieder (10), Michel Aebischer (7), Zeki Amdouni (5, 3), Johan Manzambi (3, 5). Ardon Jashari, with 1 competitive start in the window, will play at the base of midfield in Xhaka’s place (Yakin, SRF), and Akanji is expected to captain (SRF). Eight of the eleven who started the last competitive match are available. Yakin’s sides lined up 4-2-3-1 in 9 of 18 competitive matches, 4-3-3 in 4 and 3-4-3 in the first three of the 2024–25 Nations League.
  • Suspensions / absences. Switzerland: Embolo suspended, and out of the camp regardless; Xhaka, Zakaria, Amenda and Vargas out. North Macedonia: no suspension reported in any source read; Serafimov and Nikolov injured, Churlinov unavailable. Both sides play four matches in 11 days. B · Style & early-season reality

Milevski’s North Macedonia changed shape with the opponent. Against League C and D sides they had 58.2% of the ball and out-shot them 14.40 to 10.50; against League A sides they had 29.5%, took 5.40 shots against 18.00 and won 2.60 corners against 9.60, and still drew three of the five. Their goals came late: 17 of 23 after half-time, 11 of 12 at home, where the one first-half goal in seven competitive matches was a Liechtenstein own goal and five of the seven were 0–0 at the break. They were booked more than their opponents (2.53 against 1.87 per match) while committing fewer fouls (9.73 against 12.93), and at home their matches carried 5.29 cards. Miovski (6) and Bardhi (4) scored 10 of the 22; Bardhi took the one penalty kick. Milevski finished with 5-3-2 in three of his last four qualifiers. Sedloski’s side has four matches behind it, three of them friendlies, all away, none with a goal: in the three with a box score it had 38.9% of the ball, was out-shot 11 to 54 and won 4 corners against 33. Both previews read put it in a back three tonight. The evidence for Sedloski’s North Macedonia at home, and for any North Macedonia side against a League B opponent, is none; what exists is one competitive match, lost 0–4.

Yakin’s Switzerland keep the ball. They had 58.7% of possession across 16 competitive matches, out-shot opponents 12.56 to 8.88 and won more corners than the opponent in 11 of 16, crossing 17.00 times a match. They drew more fouls than they committed (12.56 against 11.12) and were booked 1.50 times a match, 0.92 since September 2025. At home they scored early (11 of 15 goals before half-time); at the opponent’s ground they did not score before half-time in six, and their one away win in that set was 2–0 in Sweden. The shape has been a 4-2-3-1 with Xhaka and Freuler as the pivot (“the duo is well-drilled”, Yakin said at the squad announcement), and the goals were spread: Embolo 6, Manzambi 5, Ndoye 4, Amdouni 3, Xhaka 3, Vargas 3. Tonight the pivot loses Xhaka to Jashari, who has one competitive start in the window, the centre-forward role, Embolo’s in 17 of the 18, goes to Boteli (uncapped), Amdouni (one start there, in Spain in November 2024) or Itten (no competitive start in the window), and the right of the back line, where none of the five players used in the window (Widmer 9 starts, Zakaria the last three) is in the squad, to Athekame or Britschgi, both uncapped, on a pitch Yakin called too long. The evidence for this Swiss eleven is the shape and the newcomers’ club form, not a match.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — both records split by opponent level, and tonight neither side meets its usual level
North Macedonia went W8 D2 L0 against League C and D sides and W0 D3 L2 against League A sides; Switzerland went W5 D2 L0 against League B sides and W0 D2 L4 against League A sides, and W1 D2 L3 at the opponent’s ground. North Macedonia have no League B-tier match to read from and no goal in four under their new coach; Switzerland are without the players behind 72 of their 198 starts and 14 of their 30 goals. The prior points both ways: promoted sides went W8 D6 L9 at home in League B but W1 D3 L4 when hosting a side relegated from League A.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — the margin records point at different sizes
North Macedonia’s seven home margins ran from 0 to +5 and they have not lost at home in the window, but their two heaviest defeats (−6 in Wales, −4 in Denmark) came against League A sides. Switzerland’s six away margins ran from −2 to +2, while against League B sides five of seven were won by two or more. A family that turns on the size of the gap inherits every level question in the 1X2 row.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — the venue lines agree under the division line; the level lines carry more goals per match
Over 2.5 ran 14.3% in North Macedonia’s seven home matches (2.14 goals) and 16.7% in Switzerland’s six away (2.17); promoted sides at home in League B ran 39.1% and hosting relegated sides 37.5%, against League B’s 50.0%. The level lines run the other way on goals per match: 3.20 in North Macedonia’s five against League A sides (over 2.5 in 40.0%, one of them 1–7) and 3.00 in Switzerland’s seven against League B sides (57.1%), set with Xhaka and Embolo.
BTTSOK — North Macedonia’s scoring half decides it
North Macedonia scored in all seven home matches but failed to score in 40.0% against League A sides and in all four under Sedloski; promoted sides hosting a relegated side failed to score in five of eight. Switzerland conceded in four of six away (clean sheet 33.3%) and kept a clean sheet in 57.1% against League B sides. BTTS ran 42.9% at North Macedonia’s home and 33.3% at Switzerland’s away grounds, against League B’s 43.8%.
Team goalsSplit — North Macedonia side OK, best on the card; Switzerland side dangerous
Every North Macedonia line that matches tonight’s level sits low: 0.60 per match against League A sides, 0.00 in four under Sedloski, 0.75 for promoted sides hosting a side relegated from League A; the 1.71 at home was scored against a set in which five of seven opponents are League C or D sides. Switzerland’s side has no stable base: 2.57 against League B sides, 1.00 against League A sides, 0.83 away, and the scorers of 14 of the 30 goals and the takers of 5 of the 7 penalty kicks are out, with an uncapped centre-forward in the predicted eleven.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK on the venue lines; 1H result dangerous
First-half goals ran 0.29 per match in North Macedonia’s seven home matches and 0.33 in Switzerland’s six away, with 9 of those 13 goalless at half-time; promoted hosts ran 0.78 (11 of 23 at 0–0) and hosts of relegated sides 0.88, against League B’s 1.12. Switzerland at home scored 11 of 15 before half-time, which is a home line, not tonight’s. The 1H result carries every 1X2 problem, plus a new Swiss pivot and a home coach in his first match in Skopje.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — asymmetric: both team records book the home side more; the official books near the division on cards and far above it on reds
North Macedonia were booked 2.53 times a match against their opponents’ 1.87, and 3.14 at home, where their matches carried 5.29 cards; Switzerland 1.50, 0.92 since September 2025, and 1.50 away, against League B’s 2.21 per team. Schlager ran 3.96 cards at 5.32 fouls per card in 52 matches, against League B’s 4.42 and 5.20, but 0.29 red cards per match against the division’s 0.06, and in his one North Macedonia match he booked five of their players. The prior is the exception: promoted hosts of relegated sides were booked 1.88 times against their visitors’ 2.12. The match total depends on a Swiss side whose low card line was set with a different midfield.
CornersDangerous — the corner split turns on the opposition’s level
North Macedonia won 4.43 and conceded 4.14 at home but won 2.60 and conceded 9.60 against League A sides, winning more in none of five; under Sedloski 1.33 against 11.00. Switzerland won 4.17 and conceded 4.33 away but 4.71 and 3.14 against League B sides. The venue lines are level and the level lines lopsided, and which applies tonight is the question the card cannot settle; promoted hosts of relegated sides won more corners in 3 of 8.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both sides step into a level their records barely cover. North Macedonia’s 15 competitive matches since September 2024 were ten against League C and D sides and five against League A sides, with none against a League B side; the two sets differ by 1.40 goals scored and 2.40 conceded per match. Switzerland’s split between their League A group and League B-tier opponents is as wide: 1.57 goals scored and 1.90 conceded per match.
  • The home coach changed after 14 of the 15 competitive matches in the strip. Sedloski has one competitive match (0–4) and three friendlies, all away, no goal, with three centre-backs in each friendly; Milevski’s side used a back four in 8 of 14. Tonight is his first match in Skopje.
  • Switzerland’s figures were set by a different spine. Xhaka (17 starts, 3 of 7 penalty kicks) and Embolo (17 starts, 6 goals) are out, with Zakaria, Vargas and Widmer; players outside the 24 made 72 of 198 starts and scored 14 of 30 goals. Jashari has one competitive start in the window; Boteli, Britschgi and Athekame none.
  • The calendar is uneven. North Macedonia last played competitively 184 days ago and last played at all 117 days ago; Switzerland 76 days ago, a World Cup quarter-final with extra time. Both play four matches in 11 days, and the away side has complained about the length of the grass.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, handicap, corners and the Switzerland side of team goals, which all rest on which level line applies and on how the new Swiss pivot and centre-forward work. Least sensitive: the North Macedonia side of team goals and the first-half goal line, where the level-matched and venue lines point the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • North Macedonia team goals are more robust than Switzerland team goals, because every North Macedonia line at tonight’s level sits between 0.00 and 0.75 per match (0.60 against League A sides, 0.00 under Sedloski, 0.75 for promoted hosts of relegated sides), while Switzerland’s runs from 0.83 to 2.57 by window and the scorers of 14 of its 30 goals are not in the squad.
  • First-half goals are more robust than full-time totals, because both venue lines sit far under League B’s 1.12 (0.29 in Skopje, 0.33 at Switzerland’s away grounds, 9 of 13 goalless at half-time), while the full-time line moves from about 2.1 goals on the venue splits to about 3.0–3.2 on the level splits.
  • BTTS is more robust than 1X2, because BTTS turns on one question, whether North Macedonia score (all seven home matches, but none of four under Sedloski and 40.0% blanks against League A sides), while 1X2 also needs a read on a Swiss side missing its captain and centre-forward.
  • The home half of Discipline is more robust than the match total, because North Macedonia’s booking line sits above League B’s 2.21 per team in the 2024–25 group, in qualifying, at home and against League A sides (2.33–3.14; 2.00 away) and Schlager booked five of them in Skopje in 2024, while Switzerland’s runs from 0.67 to 2.67 by competition and was set with Xhaka, sent off once in the window, in midfield.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is which level line applies tonight: whether Switzerland without Xhaka and Embolo play like the side that went W5 D2 against League B opposition, and whether North Macedonia at home in League B play like their League A-tier record or their home record. The Swiss half starts to settle when the elevens are published (none at 17:47 CEST); the rest needs match evidence, a box score from each side at this level, which does not yet exist and which the three matches left in this window will start to supply.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Albania are relegated from League B: fourth in Group B1 in 2024–25 on 7 points, 4–6 (W2 D1 L3, behind Czechia, Ukraine and Georgia). Their division history reads C/B/B/C, so this is a return to League C after two editions in B; they won their 2020–21 League C group, one point ahead of Belarus. In World Cup qualifying they were second in Group K on 14 points, 7–5 (W4 D2 L2, behind England, ahead of Serbia). They took four 1–0 or 3–0 wins and lost only to England, twice. In the play-off semi-final in Warsaw on 26 March they led through Arbër Hoxha (42′) and lost 1–2 (Lewandowski 63′, Zieliński 73′). Belarus stay in League C: third in Group C3 on 7 points, 3–4 (W1 D4 L1, behind Northern Ireland and Bulgaria, ahead of Luxembourg). Their history reads C/C/C/C. In World Cup qualifying they were fourth of four in Group C on 2 points, 4–17 (W0 D2 L4, including 0–6 against Denmark and 1–5 in Piraeus); they finished with 2–2 in Copenhagen and 0–0 against Greece. The structural headline is that both coaches are new and neither has taken a competitive match. Every rate in the strip was set under Sylvinho and Carlos Alós. Inside those records, 11 of Albania’s 15 competitive matches and 8 of Belarus’s 12 were against sides now in Leagues A and B. Against the rest, Albania went W3 D1, 6–1 (Latvia and Andorra), and Belarus W1 D3, 3–2 (Bulgaria and Luxembourg, all in autumn 2024). In the 17 League C matches of 2022–23 and 2024–25 in which a side just relegated from League B hosted a side that had stayed in League C, the home side went W6 D5 L6, with 3.06 goals per match, BTTS in 58.8% and the corner count to the host in 12. Belarus are in that line once, 1–1 in Slovakia in 2022–23 (baselines_0926.md). Format: group winners are promoted, runners-up play the fourth-placed League B sides, and no side is relegated from League C (baselines_0926.md, from f2). Belarus play their home matches in Budapest behind closed doors (Wikipedia’s League C page). Head to head: seven meetings, Albania W3 D2 L2, 10–10: 2–2 in Minsk (2006), 2–4 in Tirana (2007), 0–2 in Minsk (2010), 1–0 in Tirana (2011), 0–0 in Antalya (friendly, 2013), then 2–0 in Minsk and 3–2 in Tirana in the 2020–21 League C group (GitHub match record; ESPN has the two 2020 meetings, and Newsport gives the same count and goals). The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for all 27 competitive matches, set against the squads the two federations published.

  • Albania — coach Rolando Maran, appointed 19 May 2026; turnover high in the coaching and at goalkeeper, medium in the squad. Sylvinho (from 1 January 2023, 31 matches) left in May after the play-off (Wikipedia’s managers list; Sports Mole). Maran’s two matches were 0–1 at home to Israel and 0–1 at home to Luxembourg in June. Wikipedia gives him a two-year contract and sports.ru eighteen months. He named 29 on 19 September (FSHF). Four returned from injury (Rey Manaj, Ernest Muçi, Marash Kumbulla) or after two years out (Etrit Berisha), and two are uncapped (Sabah Kerjota, Samuele Sina). Out since: Qazim Laçi and Adrion Pajaziti withdrew injured, and Feta Fetai was called (Sports Mole; Wikipedia). Iván Balliu is marked injured on Wikipedia; Maran was asked why he had not been replaced at right-back, and said Sina or Ismajli could play there (Newsport). Klisman Cake has a muscle problem and is not ready (FSHF via Newsport). Maran left Simoni, Hasa, Kerjota and Fetai out of the 23 (Lajmi; Newsport). Not called: Armando Broja (form at Burnley, sports.ru) and Nedim Bajrami; Jasir Asani has not been called since March. Players outside tonight’s 23 made 69 of Albania’s 165 competitive starts since September 2024: Thomas Strakosha 15, Laçi 14, Arlind Ajeti 10 (substituted injured after 18 minutes in Warsaw; Wikipedia marks him out of this squad injured), Bajrami 9, Asani 7, Balliu 6, Broja 6 and Indrit Tuci 2. Players in the 23 scored 9 of the 11 goals that were not own goals: Kristjan Asllani 3 (one penalty), Manaj 3, Hoxha, Myrto Uzuni and Ardian Ismajli one each. In goal, none of Berisha (37, 81 caps), Alen Sherri or Mario Dajsinani has a competitive start in the window; every predicted eleven read has Berisha. Lajmi has the captain Berat Gjimshiti and Ismajli (8 starts each) at centre-back, reports that Kumbulla has not yet played for his club this season, and names Sina as the only other central-defensive option in the 23. Asllani trained apart on Wednesday and Thursday and was back with the group on Friday (FSHF via Newsport).
  • Belarus — coach Viktor Goncharenko, in charge since 6 January 2026; turnover high in the coaching and at the back. Carlos Alós left on 4 December 2025 after 26 matches, W6 D10 L10 (Wikipedia). Goncharenko signed on 6 January (ABFF and press, in the search summary); the Wikipedia statement of the date is uncited. His four matches are friendlies: 1–0 in Cyprus, 2–1 in Armenia, 4–1 against Syria and 2–2 against Burkina Faso, the last two in Minsk. He named 25 on 17 September (ABFF via Wikipedia; BelTA via Belnovosti; sports.ru): three goalkeepers, eight defenders, ten midfielders and four forwards, with Anton Kovalev and Timofey Simanenka possible debutants (sports.ru). Players outside the 25 made 58 of Belarus’s 132 competitive starts and scored 4 of the 7 goals. The most-used are centre-back Pavel Zabelin (11; last called in March), Alyaksandr Martynovich (7; not called since October 2025, and reported injured for the March camp in a search summary), Yevgeniy Shikavka (6), Vadim Pigas (5; not called since November 2025), Sergei Politevich (4, 1 goal), Yegor Parkhomenko (4; last called in June), Yury Kavalyow (3, 1 goal; left out after June, Sports Mole), and Nikita Korzun and Dmitri Antilevski (3 each). Nikita Demchenko and Gleb Kuchko each scored once. No reason for Zabelin’s, Pigas’s or Korzun’s absence was found (one search). Kept from Alós’s side: goalkeeper Fyodor Lapoukhov (11 of 12 starts), Max Ebong (11), Kiryl Pyachenin (10), Yevgeny Yablonsky and Valery Gromyko (7 each; Gromyko 2 goals) and German Barkovsky (5, the penalty in Piraeus). Nine of the 25 have no competitive start in the window, among them Gleb Shevchenko, Artem Kontsevoy and Artyom Shumansky, whom Sports Mole predicts as starters.

  • Referee. Sam Barrott (England), published on Wikipedia’s League C page with UEFA’s match link. Newsport reports the appointment of an English team, and playerstats.football lists the match as his next. ESPN’s officials field was empty when last read. Barrott refereed in the Premier League from 2023–24, joined the Select Group for 2024–25 and was added to the FIFA list in December 2024 (Wikipedia). ESPN’s record since August 2024 has him in 69 matches. In the Premier League (50) he showed 4.12 cards per match at 4.91 fouls per card, with 13 penalty kicks (0.26 per match) and 2 reds. His rate moved with the season: 4.65 in 2024–25 against a league figure of 4.21, 3.75 in 2025–26 against 3.86, and 3.00 in three matches this season against 3.84. Premier League fouls per card over those seasons ran 5.23 and 5.60, so he books at fewer fouls per card than the league’s figure, in matches with about 20 fouls, where League C averages 25.81. His eight UEFA and FIFA matches ran 3.75 cards per match: club qualifiers, an U21 qualifier, and friendlies between Trinidad and Tobago and Ghana (2025) and between Jamaica and Nigeria (2026). None is a Nations League match or a World Cup qualifier. Newsport describes a referee who lets physical duels and light contact go, does not reward simulation and is sparing with cards. That is a worded account, not a record, and it says nothing specific about contact in the box. A search summary says he had awarded the second-most Premier League penalties “this season”, without naming the season; ESPN has 7 penalty kicks in his 24 Premier League matches that season (0.29 per match, league 0.25). No history with either national side. His only match involving a Belarusian club is Maccabi Haifa 3–0 Torpedo-BelAZ Zhodino (July 2025), and there is none with an Albanian club.

Contradictions, recorded and not reconciled: Sports Mole puts the match in “League C Group 3” and its header at the Elbasan Arena, and calls the Warsaw match the play-off “finals”.
