B · Style & early-season reality

Sylvinho’s Albania were a low-event side: a 4-2-3-1 in 11 of 15, over 2.5 in 4 of 15, and seven clean sheets. Their shape moved with the opponent. Against League A and B sides they had 41.7% of the ball; against Latvia and Andorra they had 69.8%, took 13.25 shots to 5.00 and put in 21.2 crosses per match. Metaratings describes them as a low-to-mid-block side that concedes the initiative and plays on vertical counters and set pieces. ESPN’s figures support that against stronger opposition, not against the weaker sides. They foul little and are rarely booked: 9.87 fouls committed to 14.87 suffered, and 1.47 cards per match (1.00 at home, one card per 9.00 fouls). The summer changes are in goal and in the dugout. The goalkeeper behind all seven clean sheets is out, and so are Laçi (14 starts, second only to Asllani) and Ajeti, the most-used centre-back (10). Maran’s evidence is two home friendlies. In the first, ESPN records a 4-3-1-2, 59.0% of the ball, 13 shots with none on target and 24 crosses in a 0–1 defeat; the second has no box score. He calls Belarus “very compact”. He says it is not important always to have the ball but what Albania do with it, and that June taught them about reacting when they lose it (Newsport). Three predicted elevens give three different shapes.

Alós’s Belarus defended deep in a back three or five. They used a 5-3-2 five times, a 3-4-2-1 four times and a 3-4-3 once. Their only back fours were the 1–5 in Piraeus and the 1–2 at Hampden. Over 12 they had 36.9% of the ball and were outshot 9.00 to 16.67, with 2.75 shots on target to 6.75. They foul more than they are fouled (15.42 to 13.33) and are booked 2.58 times per match, 3.33 away. All seven of their goals came after half-time, and they were level or behind at every half-time. Against Bulgaria and Luxembourg in 2024 they had 40.7% of the ball but won the corner count in three of four. Those four matches carried 6.75 cards per match. Goncharenko’s shape has no match evidence. ESPN records no formation for any of his four friendlies. Sports Mole’s predicted back four includes three defenders with three competitive starts between them in the window (Malkevich 1, Shevchenko 0, Begunov 2). Goncharenko singles out Albania’s attack, naming Daku and Asllani, and says Belarus must “defend well but at the same time attack”. He also says his side has “very good balance in defence and attack” (Metaratings). Those are statements, not matches, and the defenders who played the back three are largely not in the squad.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous — both records belong to coaches who have gone, and the pairing prior is flat
Albania won three and drew one of four against sides now in League C or below (6–1); Belarus lost none of four against such sides (W1 D3, 3–2). Both records are Sylvinho’s and Alós’s. Albania’s came against two opponents, and Belarus’s all came in 2024. Relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors went W6 D5 L6 (17). Since the last competitive match, Albania have lost three friendlies without scoring, the last two under Maran, and Belarus have gone unbeaten in four under Goncharenko.
Asian handicap / DNBOK — the margin records agree on size, not on direction
11 of Albania’s 15 competitive matches finished within one goal, and all four of Belarus’s against C-or-below sides did (0, +1, 0, 0). Albania won by two or more once (3–0 against Andorra). Belarus lost by two or more only to Northern Ireland, Greece, Scotland and Denmark. Relegated-from-B hosts finished within one goal in 11 of 17. Direction is the 1X2 row’s problem and applies here in full.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — best on the card: every window at this level, and Albania’s group and venue records, sit under the division line; the pairing prior and Belarus’s matches against League A and B sides sit above it
Over 2.5 came in 4 of Albania’s 15 competitive matches (26.7%), 2 of 7 at home and 1 of 4 against C-or-below sides, and in none of Belarus’s four against such sides, against League C’s 47.9%. Albania’s qualifying xG totals were 8.6 for and 8.8 against (FotMob). Belarus’s 66.7% in qualifying and 50.0% away came against Denmark, Scotland and Greece: all three of their away overs were in Piraeus, Glasgow and Copenhagen. Relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors ran 52.9% (17). What is untested: Albania’s goalkeeper has no competitive start in the window, their most-used centre-back is out, and Belarus’s back line is largely new.
BTTSDangerous — the two halves point opposite ways, and both were built by players who are not here
Albania kept clean sheets in four of seven home matches and three of four against C-or-below sides, all with Strakosha in goal; he is out. Belarus scored in five of six away matches and three of four against C-or-below sides. Scorers of four of their seven goals are not in the squad. BTTS ran 14.3% in Albania’s home matches and 66.7% in Belarus’s away matches, against League C’s 37.5%. The pairing prior ran 58.8% (17).
Team goalsOK — both sides’ volume is steady at one or none; whether each scores at all is not
Albania scored more than once in 2 of 15 competitive matches (2–1 in Ukraine’s listed home match, 3–0 against Andorra), and Belarus in 1 of 12 (2–2 in Copenhagen). Whether each scores at all is split. Albania failed to score in four of seven at home but in none of four against C-or-below sides, and have not scored in three matches since Warsaw. Belarus failed to score in half their matches, but in only one of six away. Albania’s 23 includes the scorers of 9 of their 11 goals that were not own goals. Belarus’s 25 includes the scorers of 3 of their 7.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK on Belarus’s zero; 1H result dangerous
Belarus have not scored a first-half goal in 12 competitive matches, and Albania conceded 5 in 15 (0.33 per match), against League C’s 1.15 first-half goals per match. Albania’s own first-half scoring is the part that moves: 0.00 in their League B group, 1.00 against C-or-below sides. The half-time result follows it. Albania were level at the break in four of seven at home (all 0–0) but led in both home matches against C-or-below sides. Relegated-from-B hosts led at half-time in only 4 of 17.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — asymmetric: every line books the visitor more; the match total and the official straddle the division
Albania’s home matches ran 3.00 cards (Albania 1.00, visitors 2.00), Belarus’s away matches 4.83 (Belarus 3.33, hosts 1.50), and relegated-from-B hosts against stayed-in-C visitors 4.76 (host 1.88, visitor 2.88). Belarus commit 15.42 fouls per match to Albania’s 9.87. The total is less settled. The lines run from 3.00 to 6.75 (Belarus’s matches against C-or-below sides) around League C’s 4.85. Barrott’s Premier League rate moved from 4.65 to 3.75 between seasons, at about 4.9 fouls per card, and he has no senior competitive international in ESPN’s record.
CornersDangerous — at this level both sides won the count in three of four
Against C-or-below sides Albania won 4.00 corners to 2.75 and Belarus 4.75 to 2.50, each winning the count in three of four. Against League A and B sides Belarus won it in none of eight. Relegated-from-B hosts won it in 12 of 17 (6.29 to 2.71). Albania’s own volume is low: 3.00 won per match at home, against League C’s 4.58. Belarus won 4.83 away.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Every figure predates both coaches. Sylvinho took all 15 of Albania’s competitive matches and Alós all 12 of Belarus’s. Maran’s two friendlies were 0–1 defeats, one of them with no shot on target. Goncharenko’s four were unbeaten, against Cyprus, Armenia, Syria and Burkina Faso, with no formation or box score in ESPN’s record.
  • The personnel changes sit where the rates were built. Strakosha kept every clean sheet and is out; the three goalkeepers in the squad have no competitive start in the window. Laçi, Ajeti and Balliu (30 starts) are injured. Belarus’s back line under Alós ran through Zabelin, Martynovich and Pigas (23 starts), none of whom is in the squad. Players outside the squad scored four of Belarus’s seven goals.
  • Both records were set mostly a level up, and the level-matched evidence is thin. Eleven of Albania’s 15 and eight of Belarus’s 12 were against League A and B sides. At this level, Albania’s evidence is four matches against two opponents, and Belarus’s is four matches against two opponents two years ago. Belarus last played competitively 312 days ago.
  • The official’s record is domestic and moving. Barrott has 50 Premier League matches and no Nations League match or World Cup qualifier in ESPN’s record. His card rate fell by 0.90 per match between his two full Premier League seasons.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2, BTTS and corners, which rest on how two new coaches set up two changed defences. Least sensitive: totals and Belarus’s first-half line, where every window at this level points the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals are more robust than BTTS, because Albania’s over 2.5 rate (26.7%, 28.6% at home, 25.0% against C-or-below sides) and Belarus’s against such sides (0 of 4) all sit under League C’s 47.9%. BTTS instead turns on Albania’s conceding half, built with Strakosha in goal, and on Belarus’s scoring half, built partly by players who are not here.
  • First-half goals are more robust than the first-half result, because Belarus have no first-half goal in 12 competitive matches and Albania conceded 0.33 per match before the break. The half-time result depends on Albania’s own first-half scoring, which ran 0.00 in their League B group and 1.00 against C-or-below sides.
  • The card split is more robust than the card total, because every line reached books the visitor more: 2.00 against 1.00 in Albania’s home matches, 3.33 against 1.50 in Belarus’s away matches, 2.88 against 1.88 in the pairing prior. Belarus also commit 15.42 fouls per match to Albania’s 9.87. The total turns on whether Barrott referees like his 2024–25 Premier League season (4.65) or his 2025–26 one (3.75).
  • Margin is more robust than result, because 11 of Albania’s 15 competitive matches and all four of Belarus’s against C-or-below sides finished within one goal. The result needs a direction that two coaches without a competitive match have not yet shown.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is tonight’s two elevens. It matters most whether Goncharenko keeps the back three or five that Belarus used in 10 of 12 competitive matches, and who keeps goal for Albania. Belarus’s possession, shot and first-half lines were set in that shape by defenders now absent, and every Albanian clean sheet was Strakosha’s. The team sheets UEFA publishes about an hour before kick-off would settle both.

Slovakia – Moldova

20:45 CEST · Futbal Tatran Arena, Prešov · Two changed benches and a promoted visitor. Vladimír Weiss, re-appointed on 12 May 2026 after Francesco Calzona left in April, takes his first competitive match of his second spell. A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Moldova are promoted: League D Group D2 winners in 2024–25 on 9 points, 5–1 (W3 L1, ahead of Malta and Andorra; 2–0 and 1–0 against Andorra, 2–0 and 0–1 against Malta). Their division history reads C/D/D/C, so this is a return to League C after two editions in D. Their last League C group, 2020–21 Group C3, ended fourth on 1 point, 1–11. In World Cup qualifying they were fifth of five in Group I on 1 point, 5–32 (W0 D1 L7), including 1–11 in Oslo, after which Cleșcenco resigned. Slovakia stay in League C: second in Group C1 in 2024–25 on 13 points, 10–5 (W4 D1 L1, behind Sweden), then beaten by Slovenia in the B/C play-off (0–0 in Bratislava, 0–0 at 90 minutes and 0–1 after extra time in Ljubljana). This is their third League C edition in a row; their history reads B/C/C/C. In World Cup qualifying they were second in Group A on 12 points, 6–8 (W4 L2: 2–0 at home to Germany, 0–6 in Leipzig), then lost the play-off semi-final 3–4 at home to Kosovo. The structural headline is the gap in level between two records that both belong to earlier benches. Against sides now in League C or D, Slovakia went W6, 10–1; Moldova went W3 D1 L2, 8–5, four of those six against Malta and Andorra. Against League A and B sides Moldova lost all six, 2–28. Promoted-from-D sides took 0.33 points per away match in League C 2022–25 (W0 D4 L8 in 12) and lost all four of their matchday-one fixtures, 1–13; one of those four was Estonia 0–1 Slovakia in September 2024. At the grounds of sides that had stayed in League C, those promoted visitors went W0 D4 L4, 4–14, with one first-half goal in eight and the host winning the corner count in six. Slovakia 1–0 Estonia (November 2024) is one of the eight (baselines_0926.md). That line splits by edition: 3.50 goals per match in 2022–23, 1.00 in 2024–25. Format: group winners are promoted, runners-up play the fourth-placed League B sides, and no side is relegated from League C (baselines_0926.md, from f2). Head to head: two meetings in the GitHub match record, both 2002 World Cup qualifiers: 1–0 to Slovakia in Chișinău (October 2000) and 4–2 in Trenčín (September 2001). Sports Mole adds a 2–0 Moldova win in a February 2000 friendly that the GitHub record does not hold. The RESEARCH SPEC’s transfer-fee field has no national-team equivalent. Turnover below is read from ESPN’s starting elevens for all 27 competitive matches, set against the squads the two federations published.

  • Slovakia — coach Vladimír Weiss, re-appointed 12 May 2026; turnover high on the bench, low in the squad, concentrated at right-back and in midfield. Calzona (from 30 August 2022) left after the Kosovo defeat. Wikipedia’s national-team page ends his tenure on 21 April 2026 and his own page on 28 April; Sports Mole says he was relieved of his duties in April. Weiss coached Slovakia from 2008 to 2012, including the 2010 World Cup, then Georgia (2016–20) and Slovan Bratislava (2021–26). His two matches are friendlies in June: 2–1 against Malta in Dunajská Streda and 2–2 against Montenegro in Košice. He named 30 on 17 September (SFZ), five from the domestic league and one uncapped player, Nino Marcelli. He says the group “must” be won. Out since: Matúš Bero (10 competitive starts, all but one on the right of midfield) withdrew with a stress fracture in his foot and needs surgery (TASR via Sportnet). Hugo Pavek, Nino Marcelli and Mario Sauer played for the U21 side against Ireland in Košice on Friday (lost 0–2). Weiss said they would probably not have made the 20-man matchday squad and would likely rejoin afterwards. Sports Mole and Sportnet both predicted Pavek at right-back. Not in the 30: Norbert Gyömbér (11 starts, 10 at right-back; last called in March, no reason found), Peter Pekarík (3 starts; retired from international football after his 138th cap in June, per Sports Mole), László Bénes (5 starts; last called in June), Marek Rodák (2 starts; injured and back in training, per Weiss), Patrik Hrošovský (retired; his farewell is tonight). Aleksandar Čavrič declined the call-up for personal reasons. Players outside the 29 still in the squad made 34 of Slovakia’s 165 competitive starts (Bero 10 of them) and scored none of their 18 goals that were not own goals; David Strelec scored 6, Dávid Hancko and Lukáš Haraslín 2 each. Kept from Calzona’s side: Hancko (14 starts), Milan Škriniar, Stanislav Lobotka and Ondrej Duda (13 each), Dúbravka (12 in goal), Strelec (11). Weiss has not named a starting goalkeeper. He said he would not use the same one in all four matches, and Dúbravka is currently second choice at Tottenham. Weiss also says several attackers lack club minutes: Strelec, Róbert Boženík, Tomáš Bobček, Tomáš Suslov and Tomáš Rigo.
  • Moldova — coach Lilian Popescu, appointed 16 September 2025; turnover high in attack, at centre-back and in goal. Serghei Cleșcenco resigned on 11 September 2025, two days after the 1–11 in Oslo (FMF via Wikipedia). Popescu, previously Moldova U19, Zimbru (2022–24) and Petrocub (2017–22), has eight matches, W0 D3 L5. His three competitive matches were 1–1 in Tallinn, 0–2 at home to Italy (both goals after the 87th minute) and 1–4 against Israel after Perciun was sent off in the 18th minute. His friendlies were 1–2 in Romania, 0–2 against Lithuania, 2–3 in Cyprus, 2–2 against Bulgaria and 1–1 in Armenia. He named 25 (FMF, 14–15 September), of whom 24 travelled. Ioan-Călin Revenco (2 starts; last season at Tatran Prešov) and Nicolae Rotaru are injured, and Serghei Țurcan was called in (Jurnal.md, 25 September). Virgiliu Postolachi (9 starts, 1 goal) withdrew injured (Sports Mole), and Victor Bogaciuc has been injured since July. Not in the squad, with no reason found: Ion Nicolaescu (8 starts, 3 goals; Moldova’s record scorer), Mihail Caimacov (11 starts, 2 goals), Artur Ioniță (7, 1 goal), all last called in November 2025; Artur Crăciun (10 starts at centre-back; withdrew in March), Sergiu Plătică (7) and Victor Mudrac (6). The Moldovan searches did not find the reasons. Players outside the 24 made 70 of Moldova’s 132 competitive starts and scored 8 of the 9 goals that were not own goals. Kept: Oleg Reabciuk (11 starts, back after a long absence per Radio Moldova), Vadim Rață and captain Vladislav Baboglo (10 each), Nichita Moțpan (6), Celeadnic (5 in goal). Nine of the 24 have no competitive start in the window, including both forwards named in the predicted elevens, Dmitri Mandrîcenco and Petru Popescu.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side in any source read. Perciun’s red card against Israel (November 2025) is Moldova’s last competitive dismissal. He is in the travelling 24 (Jurnal.md; FMF), while Wikipedia marks him as standby for this match; no source read says he is suspended. Slovakia: Bero out; Rodák injured; Pavek, Marcelli and Mario Sauer played for the U21s on Friday. Moldova: Revenco, Rotaru, Postolachi and Bogaciuc injured. Both sides play four matches in 11 days. Slovakia host Kazakhstan in Košice (29 September), then go to Tórshavn (2 October) and Chișinău (6 October). Moldova host Faroe Islands (29 September), go to Astana (2 October) and host Slovakia (6 October) (Wikipedia’s League C page; SFZ; Jurnal.md).

  • Referee. Juxhin Xhaja (Albania), published on Wikipedia’s League C page with UEFA’s match link. TASR (via Sportnet) and Jurnal.md name the same official and his full Albanian team. ESPN’s officials field was empty when last read. He is 36, has refereed in the Albanian top flight since October 2016 and has been on the FIFA list since 2018. He was the FSHF’s referee of the year for 2024 (Wikipedia). ESPN’s record since August 2024 has him in 18 matches, 17 with card data: 84 cards, 4.94 per match, 3 reds, and 5 penalty kicks (0.29 per match). The 15 with a box score give 4.42 fouls per card. By season he showed 4.57 cards per match in 2024–25 (7), 5.38 in 2025–26 (8) and 4.50 in 2026–27 (2). Fifteen are UEFA club matches: Europa League, Conference League and qualifiers. His two competitive national-team matches, Estonia 0–3 Sweden in League C (October 2024; Slovakia’s group) and Liechtenstein 0–1 Wales (November 2025), had 5 cards each on 26 and 14 fouls. A friendly, Kosovo 3–0 Andorra (June 2026), has no card record in ESPN. playerstats.football gives him 5.00 yellows per match in 19 Albanian Superliga matches in 2023/24, 4.40 in 20 in 2022/23 and 4.47 in 17 in 2021/22. It lists no Superliga match in 2024/25 and one in 2025/26 (9 yellows), and gives a career line of 4.56 yellows and 0.14 reds per match. Against League C’s 4.85 cards and 5.32 fouls per card, he books at the division’s rate or a little above it, and at fewer fouls per card. No history with either national side, and no Slovak or Moldovan club match in ESPN’s record. No worded account of how he reads contact in the box was found.

B · Style & early-season reality

Calzona’s Slovakia played a 4-3-3 in 13 of 15 competitive matches. The exceptions were a back three against Northern Ireland and Kosovo, both in the last three matches. Their possession moved with the opponent: 30.3% and 28.1% against Germany, 53.7% against C-or-below sides, and 50.7% over 15. At this level they controlled the shot count rather than the ball: 15.83 shots to 7.83 and 5.50 on target to 2.33 against Estonia, Azerbaijan and Luxembourg, with 49.3% of the ball in the three home matches among those six. They foul without being booked: 12.00 fouls per match at 7.64 fouls per card (1.53 cards per match), against 14.93 fouls and 2.40 cards from their opponents. 11 of their 19 goals came after half-time, and four from the 76th minute. The summer change is on the bench and at right-back. Weiss’s evidence is two friendlies with no box score, both recorded as 4-3-3. He says Moldova are “well organised in defensive activity” and that Slovakia “must be faster, more dynamic and technically more skilled”. Škriniar expects Moldova to sit “in a closed block, waiting for counters and our mistakes” (TASR via Sportnet). The right-back position, where Gyömbér and Pekarík made all 13 starts, has no incumbent in the matchday pool beyond a U21 player who played on Friday. That is a squad list, not a match.

Moldova defended with a back three or five in 11 of 12 competitive matches: 5-4-1 five times, 5-3-2 four times, 3-5-2 and 3-5-1-1 once each. The only back four was the 2–3 at home to Estonia. Their shape held, but their share of the game moved with the level. Against League D sides and Estonia they had 50.6% of the ball, took 13.00 shots to 9.83 and won the corner count in five of six. Against League A and B sides they had 31.3%, were outshot 8.17 to 26.00 and won it in none of six. They foul more than they are fouled at this level: 17.17 fouls committed per match against C-or-below sides, 19.33 away from home against them, and 3.33 cards per match. They concede early and late: 5 of 33 goals inside the first 15 minutes and 10 from the 76th. Popescu’s three competitive matches kept the back five and gave 29.8% possession, 7.67 shots to 23.67 and 2.67 corners to 8.33, all against Group I opposition. Weiss says Popescu “improved the organisation” and that Moldova’s defending has been better recently. Popescu frames tonight as getting past obstacles, “first of all mental ones” (Moldfootball headline). The attacking evidence is the weakest part: the scorers of 8 of the 9 goals are not here, and the two predicted forwards have no competitive start in the window.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — the level-matched records agree on direction; the draw share is the open part
Slovakia went W6 against sides now in League C or below (10–1); stayed-in-C hosts against promoted-from-D visitors went W4 D4 L0 (8); Moldova have not won in 15. Every one of those Slovak results was Calzona’s, and 9 of Moldova’s 12 competitive matches were Cleșcenco’s. The draws are the unsettled part. Half the pairing prior were draws, 4 of 8, two in each edition. Moldova drew 1–1 in Tallinn under Popescu, and Slovakia drew twice at home in the window.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous — the margin records split by opposition level
All six of Slovakia’s wins against C-or-below sides were by one or two goals, and Moldova lost by more than one in none of their six matches at that level. Against League A and B sides Moldova lost all six by two or more (2–28). The pairing prior’s margins were 0, 0, 0, 0, +1, +1, +4, +4. Which of Moldova’s two records applies to a side that beat Germany and lost to Kosovo is the question the margin rests on.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — best on the card: every level-matched window sits under the division line
Over 2.5 came in 1 of Slovakia’s 6 matches against C-or-below sides (none of 3 at home) and 1 of Moldova’s 6 (none of 3 away, none of 4 in League D), against League C’s 47.9%. The pairing prior ran 37.5%: 3 of 4 in 2022–23, none of 4 in 2024–25. Slovakia’s home rate is 25.0% (8). The windows above the line are Moldova’s against League A and B sides (66.7%), where they conceded 27.6 xG in qualifying. What is untested: a Moldovan defence without Crăciun and Mudrac (16 starts at centre-back), and a Slovak attack whose coach says it lacks club minutes.
BTTSOK — asymmetric: the Slovak clean-sheet half is steady; the Moldovan scoring half was built by absent players
Slovakia kept clean sheets in 5 of 6 against C-or-below sides and 6 of 8 at home; stayed-in-C hosts kept 5 of 8. Every window at this level runs at or under League C’s 37.5% BTTS: Slovakia 16.7%, Moldova 33.3%, pairing prior 37.5%. Moldova scored in five of six against C-or-below sides, but the scorers of 8 of their 9 goals that were not own goals are not in the squad. Slovakia’s goalkeeper is also undecided; Dúbravka kept 8 of the 9 clean sheets.
Team goalsSplit — Slovakia’s side reads; Moldova’s dangerous
Slovakia scored in all six against C-or-below sides, one to three goals each time (10 in 6), with every one of their 18 non-own goals in the window scored by players still in the squad. Promoted-from-D visitors conceded 1.75 per match at stayed-in-C grounds. Moldova’s side has no usable base. Their scorers of 8 of 9 goals are absent. The 24 have scored one competitive goal in the window between them (Bodișteanu in Tallinn), and the predicted front two have no competitive start.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — Moldova’s first-half zero reads; the host’s first half and the half-time result do not
Moldova have not scored a first-half goal in five competitive matches away from Chișinău, and promoted-from-D visitors scored one in eight at stayed-in-C grounds. Slovakia’s own first half is the part that moves. They were level at half-time in 5 of 6 against C-or-below sides (four at 0–0) and scored 0.50 first-half goals per match there. The pairing prior’s hosts scored 1.12, all but one of those goals in 2022–23. League C runs 1.15 first-half goals per match.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — asymmetric: every line books Moldova more; the total straddles the division
Slovakia take 1.53 cards per match at 7.64 fouls per card; Moldova take 2.42 at 5.69, and 3.33 against C-or-below sides. In Slovakia’s home matches visitors took 2.62 cards to Slovakia’s 1.25. The total is less settled. Slovakia’s home matches ran 3.88, and so did the pairing prior (host 1.88, visitor 2.00). Moldova’s matches against C-or-below sides ran 6.50, and League C runs 4.85. Xhaja’s 17 recorded matches ran 4.94 cards at 4.42 fouls per card, with two national-team matches at 5 each.
CornersDangerous — Moldova’s corner record flips with the level of opponent
Against C-or-below sides Moldova won the corner count in five of six (3.50 to 2.50); against League A and B sides in none of six (3.33 to 7.33), and 2.67 to 8.33 under Popescu. Slovakia won it in 4 of 6 against C-or-below sides (5.83 to 4.00) and 5 of 8 at home. Stayed-in-C hosts won it in 6 of 8 (5.62 to 2.62). The direction depends on which Moldova turns up.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both benches changed after most of the strip was recorded. All 15 of Slovakia’s competitive matches were Calzona’s. Weiss has two friendlies, with no box score, and no competitive match. Nine of Moldova’s 12 were Cleșcenco’s. Popescu’s three were all against Group I sides (Estonia, Italy, Israel), and his eight matches have produced no win.
  • Moldova’s rates were built by players who are not here. Players outside the 24 made 70 of 132 competitive starts and scored 8 of 9 non-own goals. Both regular centre-backs, Crăciun and Mudrac (16 starts), are among them, and so is Cristian Avram, who kept goal in three of the qualifiers. On Slovakia’s side the change is narrower but sits in specific places: the right-back pair (13 starts), Bero (10) and the goalkeeper choice.
  • The level-matched evidence is thin and uneven. Moldova’s six matches against C-or-below sides include four against Malta and Andorra, now League D. Slovakia’s six are Estonia, Azerbaijan and Luxembourg. The pairing prior is eight matches whose two editions point in opposite directions: 3.50 goals per match in 2022–23, 1.00 in 2024–25. Moldova last played competitively 314 days ago.
  • The official’s record is mostly club football. Xhaja has two competitive national-team matches in ESPN’s record against 15 UEFA club matches. His domestic figures stop at one Superliga match since 2023/24.
  • Most sensitive: Moldova’s team goals, the margin families (Asian handicap) and corners, which depend on which Moldova record applies with a changed squad. Least sensitive: totals and Moldova’s first-half line, where every level-matched window points the same way.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Totals are more robust than the Asian handicap, because every level-matched window sits under League C’s 47.9% over 2.5: Slovakia 16.7% against C-or-below sides and 25.0% at home, Moldova 16.7% and none of four in League D. The handicap instead turns on whether Moldova’s one-goal defeats at this level or their six heavy defeats against League A and B sides describe them against Slovakia.
  • Moldova’s first-half line is more robust than the half-time result, because they have no first-half goal in five competitive matches away from Chișinău, and promoted-from-D visitors scored one in eight at stayed-in-C grounds. The half-time result depends on Slovakia’s own first half, which was level at the break in 5 of 6 at this level.
  • The card split is more robust than the card total, because every line reached books Moldova more: 2.42 cards per match against Slovakia’s 1.53, 3.33 against C-or-below sides, and 2.62 for visitors in Slovakia’s home matches. The total turns on whether the match runs like Slovakia’s home matches (3.88) or Moldova’s matches at this level (6.50), with an official at 4.94.
  • Slovakia’s team goals are more robust than Moldova’s, because Slovakia scored in all six against C-or-below sides with every scorer of their 18 non-own goals in tonight’s squad. The scorers of 8 of Moldova’s 9 are absent.
  • The single fact that would most change which family reads best is Moldova’s team sheet: whether Popescu keeps the back five used in 11 of 12 competitive matches, who plays at centre-back in place of Crăciun and Mudrac, and who plays up front. Their corner, margin and scoring records at this level were set by that shape with players now absent. UEFA’s team sheets, about an hour before kick-off, will settle it. The first League C box scores (Moldova at home to Faroe Islands on 29 September) will show what the shape produces.

Across the card

  • Best structurally supported: Discipline, OK or split on all ten fixtures, every row reading a published appointment against both team lines; Team goals, OK at Albania–Belarus and split on the other nine, with at least one side readable on each; Totals, Well-suited at Bulgaria–Luxembourg, OK or split on eight more, and best on the card at Albania–Belarus and Slovakia–Moldova; BTTS, OK on seven of ten.
  • Least supported: the 1X2, Dangerous at Slovenia–Scotland, Faroe Islands–Kazakhstan, Bulgaria–Luxembourg, Czechia–Croatia, England–Spain, North Macedonia–Switzerland and Albania–Belarus; Corners, Dangerous at Slovenia–Scotland, Faroe Islands–Kazakhstan, Bulgaria–Luxembourg, Czechia–Croatia, North Macedonia–Switzerland, Albania–Belarus and Slovakia–Moldova; the Asian handicap, Dangerous on five of ten; BTTS at Czechia–Croatia, England–Spain and Albania–Belarus; Halves at Czechia–Croatia and England–Spain; Totals at England–Spain, where the windows run from 1.75 to 4.43 goals per match.
  • No normal fixture. Every card carries at least one Dangerous verdict. San Marino–Finland and Iceland–Estonia come closest, with one each, on the Asian handicap.
  • Thinnest evidence: Slovenia–Scotland, Albania–Belarus and Slovakia–Moldova, where the coaches changed after most figures in the strip were recorded and the cards are flagged high-uncertainty; North Macedonia–Switzerland, also flagged, where the home coach has one competitive match and players outside Switzerland’s squad made 72 of their 198 competitive starts; Faroe Islands–Kazakhstan, where the referee’s ESPN record is eleven matches, ten of them club matches and only four with a box score; Bulgaria–Luxembourg, built at reduced depth.

Scope. This page rates market families only. It contains no selections, no sides, no recommended lines and no confidence labels — by design. Where evidence is thin it says so rather than filling the gap.
